Hassett–Keel Program in genus four
Kenneth Ascher, Kristin DeVleming, Yuchen Liu, Xiaowei Wang

Abstract
. The Hassett–Keel program seeks to give a modular interpretation to the steps of the log minimal model program of ℳ ¯ g \overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g} . The goal of this paper is to complete the Hassett–Keel program in genus four, supplementing earlier results of Casalaina-Martin–Jensen–Laza and Alper–Fedorchuk–Smyth–van der Wyck. The main tools we use are wall crossing for moduli spaces of pairs in the sense of K-stability and KSBA stability, and the recently constructed moduli spaces of boundary polarized Calabi–Yau surface pairs. We also give a construction of the hyperelliptic flip using a stackified Chow quotient which is expected to generalize to higher genus.



1.
Introduction

The
Hassett–Keel program
asks whether the steps of the log minimal model program on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
, the Deligne–Mumford moduli space of stable curves of genus
g\geq 2
, have natural modular interpretations. More specifically, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
denote the moduli stack and consider the divisor
K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta
, where
\delta=\delta_{0}+\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{\lfloor g/2\rfloor}

is the sum of the boundary divisors on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
. If
\phi:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\to\overline{M}_{g}
is the coarse moduli map and
\Delta_{i}
the associated boundary divisor to
\delta_{i}
on
\overline{M}_{g}
, then

K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta=\phi^{*}\left(K_{\overline{M}_{g}}+\alpha(\Delta_{0}+\Delta_{2}+\dots+\Delta_{\lfloor g/2\rfloor})+\tfrac{1+\alpha}{2}\Delta_{1}\right).

For any rational number
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta
is big, by
[
BCHM10
]
the log canonical ring is finitely generated and we may form the log canonical model

\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)=\Proj\left(\oplus_{m\geq 0}H^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g},\lfloor m(K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta)\rfloor)\right).

For
\alpha=1
, the divisor
K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\delta
is known to be ample (
[
Mum77
]
,
[
Has05
, Theorem 5.7]
), so
\overline{M}_{g}(1)=\overline{M}_{g}
. Hassett asked if
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
admits a modular interpretation for all
\alpha\in(0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
.

In general, the Hassett–Keel program is understood when
\alpha>\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon

(see Section
2.3
for more details, differences for
g\in\{2,3\}
and discussion of terminology). Indeed, Hassett–Hyeon
[
HH09
,
HH13
]
show that if
\alpha\in(\frac{9}{11},1)
, then
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)=\overline{M}_{g}
. At
\alpha=\frac{9}{11}
, there is a non-trivial birational modification, a divisorial contraction, contracting
elliptic tails
to cuspidal (
A_{2}
) singularities. For
\alpha\in(\frac{7}{10},\frac{9}{11}]
, there is an isomorphism
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)\cong\overline{M}_{g}^{\mathrm{ps}}
, where
\overline{M}_{g}^{\mathrm{ps}}
denotes the moduli space of Schubert pseudostable curves
[
Sch91
]
. When
g\geq 3
, the authors show that the moduli space undergoes a flip at
\alpha=\frac{7}{10}
corresponding to the replacement of
elliptic bridges
by tacnodal (
A_{3}
) singularities. When
g\geq 4
, Alper, Fedorchuk, Smyth, and van der Wyck
[
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
,
AFS17b
]
showed that there is a flip at
\alpha=\frac{2}{3}
, corresponding to the replacement of Weierstrass genus two tails by ramphoid cuspidal (
A_{4}
) singularities. Note that Hyeon and Lee in
[
HL14
]
showed that there is no wall crossing for
g=4
when
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3},\frac{7}{10})
, which was generalized by
[
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
,
AFS17b
]
to any
g\geq 4
.

When
g=4
, the range
\alpha\leq\frac{5}{9}
is also understood: see
[
CMJL14
]
and
[
Fed12
,
CMJL12
]
for partial earlier results. By VGIT for
(2,3)
-complete intersection curves in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
,
[
CMJL14
]
shows there are three critical values of
\alpha
(and one additional terminal value) in the range
\alpha<\frac{5}{9}
for which the moduli spaces change, corresponding to the last four rows of Table
1
.

The purpose of this paper is to complete the Hassett–Keel program for genus four curves by proving the following. Below, the notation
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(b,a)
(respectively,
\overline{M}_{g}(b,a)
) for rational numbers
b>a
represents
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
(respectively,
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
) for any
\alpha\in(a,b)
.

Theorem 1.1
.

For each rational number
\alpha\in[\frac{5}{9},\frac{2}{3})
, there exists a smooth algebraic stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
of finite type with affine diagonal that parameterizes
\alpha
-stable curves of genus four, which are projective curves of genus four with lci singularities and ample canonical bundle satisfying certain conditions (see Definition
7.10
).
The stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
admits a projective good moduli space isomorphic to
\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha)
, the log canonical model of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}
. Furthermore, these stacks and good moduli spaces satisfy the following wall crossing diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3},\frac{19}{29})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{19}{29})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{19}{29},\frac{5}{9})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{2}{3},\frac{19}{29})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{19}{29})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{19}{29},\frac{5}{9})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the arrows in the top row are open immersions, the vertical arrows are good moduli space morphisms, and the arrows in the bottom row are projective birational morphisms.

•

As
\alpha
decreases from
\alpha=\frac{19}{29}+\epsilon
to
\alpha=\frac{19}{29}
, there is a contraction of the divisor
\Delta_{2}
parametrizing the union of two genus two curves intersecting at a non-Weierstrass point of each curve, with image parametrizing curves with a separating
A_{5}
singularity.

•

As
\alpha
decreases from
\alpha=\frac{5}{9}+\epsilon
to
\alpha=\frac{5}{9}
, the locus of curves with elliptic triboroughs (two genus one curves meeting at three points) is contracted to the locus of curves with
D_{4}
singularities; the locus of hyperelliptic curves is contracted to a point parametrizing the unique hyperelliptic ribbon, and the locus of curves on quadric cones with a tacnodal singularity at the vertex of the cone is contracted to a curve
\Gamma\subset\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
.

Combining this result with
[
CMJL14
]
, which verifies the Hassett–Keel program in genus four for
\alpha\leq\frac{5}{9}
and
[
HH09
,
HH13
,
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
,
AFS17b
]
, which verify the Hassett–Keel program in all genus for
\alpha>\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon
, we obtain the full Hassett–Keel program for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The full Hassett–Keel program for
g=4
has the following description.

(1)

The critical values of
\alpha
(called
walls
) at which the moduli stacks change are

(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{9})=\left(\frac{9}{11},\frac{7}{10},\frac{2}{3},\frac{19}{29},\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44},\frac{1}{2},\frac{29}{60},\frac{8}{17}\right).

For each
\alpha_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 9
, there is a sequence of stacks and good moduli spaces satisfying the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})}

If
i=1
, we take
\alpha_{0}=1
, and if
i=9
, we take
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha_{9},\alpha_{10})=\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha_{9},\alpha_{10})=\emptyset
. For
i=9
, the moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha_{9})=\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{8}{17})=\{*\}
is the terminal model of
\overline{M}_{4}
.

(2)

The curve singularities parameterized by
\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha)
are as predicted in Table
1
.

Remark 1.3

(Previous expectations)
.

In
[
AFS16
, Section 3]
, the authors predict values corresponding to walls for
\alpha>\frac{5}{9}
for general genus
g
and in
[
CMJL14
]
, the authors predict values for the range
(\frac{2}{3},\frac{5}{9})
for
g=4
. The known walls and predictions are described in Table
1
, with the previously unconfirmed walls emphasized in bold. In the table, the notation
A_{2n+1}^{\rm sep}
(resp.
A_{2n+1}^{\rm nsep}
) is used to denote curves with
separating

A_{2n+1}
singularities, where normalization of the singularity disconnects the curve (resp.
non-separating

A_{2n+1}
singularities, where the curve remains connected after normalization of the singularity).

Our results are mostly in line with previous predictions (see
[
Fed12
,
AFS16
,
CMJL14
]
), aside from the case of
A_{6}
singularities, which were predicted to be replaced at
\alpha=\frac{49}{83}
.

1.1.
Method of proof

To prove Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
, we first compare
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
and
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
, known by the earlier works
[
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
,
AFS17b
]
and
[
CMJL14
]
. Comparing these two known moduli spaces, one observes that they are isomorphic away from three disjoint loci in
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
: a point
[C_{2A_{5}}]
parametrizing the
(2,3)
-complete intersection curve
C_{2A_{5}}
in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
with two separating
A_{5}
singularities, a point
[C_{D}]
parametrizing the
(2,3)
-complete intersection curve
C_{D}
with two
D_{4}
singularities and three
A_{1}
singularities, and a curve
\Gamma
parametrizing certain singular
(2,3)
-complete intersection curves
C_{A,B}
(which generically have both
A_{5}
and
A_{3}
singularities) on singular quadric surfaces (see
[
CMJL12
, Theorems 3.1 & 4.1]
).

We verify the predictions of the Hassett–Keel program by studying each of these three loci separately. More precisely, in an open neighborhood of each point, we construct stacks and good moduli spaces satisfying a wall crossing diagram that will be glued together to form the global wall crossings for
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
. For the isolated points
[C_{2A_{5}}]
and
[C_{D}]
, we use the recent theory of wall crossing for boundary polarized Calabi–Yau pairs from
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
(see Section
2.5
and Section
6
). For the curve
\Gamma
introduced in Theorem
1.1
, we construct a stack
\mathscr{X}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
directly and a suitable open substack
\mathscr{U}^{+}\subset\mathscr{X}
of curves that admit degenerations to curves parameterized by
\Gamma
that will serve as a local model of the wall crossing
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9}+\epsilon)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
(see Section
5
). We prove that the stacks admit good moduli spaces, and then glue together the relevant substacks in a neighborhood of each locus to form the stacks
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
as
\alpha
varies from
\frac{5}{9}
to
\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon
(see Theorem
7.12
). Finally, by verifying that the log canonical divisor
K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}}+\alpha\delta
descends to an ample line bundle on the good moduli space (see Theorem
7.15
), we conclude that the good moduli space is the log canonical model of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}
.

1.2.
Wall crossing for pairs

As mentioned above, we construct the wall crossing that replaces the curves
C_{2A_{5}}
and
C_{D}
using the theory of wall crossing for
boundary polarized Calabi–Yau pairs
(bpCYs) as developed in
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
.

If
X
is a Fano variety and
D
is an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
such that
(X,D)
is slc and
K_{X}+D\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}0
, we may consider moduli of spaces of pairs
(X,cD)
(and their
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein degenerations) for all rational coefficients
c>0
such that
(X,cD)
is slc. For
c<1
, the pair
(X,cD)
is log Fano and the associated moduli spaces can be constructed using K-stability
[
Xu25
]
. These moduli spaces admit a wall crossing framework with respect to the coefficient
c
by
[
ADL24
,
Zho23
]
. For
c>1
, the pair
(X,cD)
is log canonically polarized and the associated moduli spaces can be constructed using KSBA stability
[
Kol23
]
and also admit a wall crossing framework with respect to
c
by
[
ABIP23
,
MZ23
]
. Finally, for
c=1
, in certain settings, the works
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
connect the K-moduli and KSBA moduli spaces with an additional boundary polarized Calabi–Yau moduli space. We note that connections between wall crossing and the Hassett–Keel program have already been studied in the case of genus
g=3
; see for example
[
ADL24
, Section 9.3.1]
and
[
HL10
]
.

In this paper, we apply this theory to the case where
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a quadric surface and
D=\frac{2}{3}C
for
C
a
(2,3)
-complete intersection curve, and consider bpCY degenerations of
(X,D)
. The curves
C_{2A_{5}}
and
C_{D}
each can be viewed in this context as bpCY pairs
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}})
and
(\mathbb{P}^{2}\cup\mathbb{P}^{2},\frac{2}{3}C_{D})
, and the bpCY wall crossing in an open neighborhood of each point provides a local model for the Hassett–Keel program. More precisely, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
denote the bpCY moduli stack of all pairs
(X,D)
(see Definition
2.29
). Then it follows from
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
admits a projective good moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
. Note that in our setting, the stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is already bounded as it does not contain type III bpCY pairs by
[
ABB+23
, Theorem 16.11]
.
Then we prove that there is an open neighborhood
\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{CY}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
of the above two bpCY pairs associated to
C_{2A_{5}}
and
C_{D}
such that the forgetful map
[(X,D)]\mapsto[D]
is an isomorphism onto its image inside the stack of Gorenstein curves (Theorem
6.18
). In a neighborhood of
[C_{2A_{5}}]
and
[C_{D}]
, we use open substacks of
\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{K}}\subset\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{CY}}
and
\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}\subset\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{CY}}
and the associated bpCY wall crossing to realize the Hassett–Keel program. By analysis of the pairs
(X,D)
parameterized by
\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{K}},\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{KSBA}},
and
\mathscr{W}^{\mathrm{CY}}
, one proves directly that
D
is an
\alpha
-stable curve for the appropriate
\alpha
in the Hassett–Keel program (Propositions
6.7
and
6.12
) and therefore suitable open substacks give local models of the Hassett–Keel program near
[C_{2A_{5}]}
and
[C_{D}]
.

We note that this bpCY wall crossing does
not
give the appropriate wall crossing in the Hassett–Keel program in a neighborhood of the third locus
\Gamma
, which must be replaced using a separate method. Indeed, the KSBA moduli space of
(X,(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)C)
on one side of the bpCY wall crossing compactifies the
blowup
of
M_{4}
along the hyperelliptic locus by
[
DH21
, Theorem 2]
, and thus cannot be a log canonical model of
\overline{M}_{4}
. In the K-moduli space of
(X,(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)C)
on the other side of the bpCY wall crossing, by
[
ADL23
, Remark 6.10]
, the walls in the Hassett–Keel program for
\alpha<\frac{5}{9}
obtained by VGIT are a strict proper subset of the K-moduli walls and thus this K-moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
does not agree with any of the Hassett–Keel models.

We also note that the wall crossing for K-moduli spaces has applications to the Hassett–Keel program in genus six, see
[
Zha24
,
Zha23
]
. In addition, one of the Hassett–Keel models in genus four appears naturally as a K-moduli space of Fano threefolds, see
[
LZ24
]
.

Finally, we prove the following result of independent interest relating moduli spaces of genus four curves and K3 surfaces which is a slight improvement of
[
BL24
, Theorem 7.11]
.

Theorem 1.4

(see Theorem
6.16
)
.

The moduli stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is smooth. Moreover, its good moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is a normal projective variety and isomorphic to the Baily–Borel compactification of Kondō’s ball quotient from
[
Kon02
]
.

1.3.
The hyperelliptic flip and the stackified Chow quotient

The remaining locus to study in
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
is a curve
\Gamma
parameterizing a pencil
C_{A,B}
of singular curves on a quadric cones, containing a unique point representing the hyperelliptic ribbon (see Definition
2.18
), all of which are
\alpha
-unstable for
\alpha>\frac{5}{9}
. Thus, we wish to construct the wall crossing for the Hassett–Keel program in a neighborhood of this curve
\Gamma
. By standard predictions in the Hassett–Keel program, the hyperelliptic ribbon must be replaced (as
\alpha
increases) by all hyperelliptic genus four curves, and the wall crossing interchanging the hyperelliptic curves and ribbons is called the
hyperelliptic flip
.

In
[
CMJL12
,
CMJL14
]
, the authors prove that
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
is isomorphic to the Chow quotient
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}_{4}
of cycles associated to
(2,3)
-complete intersection curves (see Theorem
2.22
). The authors conjectured (see, e.g.
[
CMJL12
, Last paragraph of Section 4]
) that this Chow quotient would provide a solution to the open problem of constructing the hyperelliptic flip. We ultimately verify this conjecture based on work in Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
. To do so, for our wall crossing framework, we first need a stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
of curves with open immersions
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9}\pm\epsilon)\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
admitting the Chow quotient as a good moduli space. The Chow scheme only parameterizes
cycles
, and so we construct a new stack
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{X}_{4}
of curves which presents a “stackification” of the Chow quotient and prove that it admits a good moduli space isomorphic to the Chow quotient (see Remark
2.23
) which enables us to set
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})=\mathscr{X}_{4}
. We also note that our stack
\mathscr{X}_{4}
is similar in spirit to the moduli stack of branchvarieties from
[
HLFHJR24
]
.

We first consider a stack
\mathscr{X}_{g}
parameterizing smoothable Gorenstein genus
g
curves
C
such that
\omega_{C}
is ample and basepoint free, and that the Hilbert–Chow image of
C
induced by
|\omega_{C}|
is a Chow-semistable
1
-cycle in
\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
(see Definition
4.2
for a precise definition).

Theorem 1.5

(see Theorem
4.3
)
.

Let
g\geq 3
be an integer. Then
\mathscr{X}_{g}
is an irreducible algebraic stack of finite type with affine diagonal that contains
\mathcal{M}_{g}
as a dense open substack. Moreover,
\mathscr{X}_{g}
contains a smooth open neighborhood of the point corresponding to the unique hyperelliptic ribbon of genus
g
.

The main purpose of the stack
\mathscr{X}_{g}
is to (potentially) provide a description of the hyperelliptic flip in the Hassett–Keel program for all
g
. Toward this goal, we define stacks of hyperelliptic curves
\mathscr{H}_{g}
and ribbons
\mathscr{R}_{g}
(see Definition
3.12
) and give explicit presentations of these stacks as quotient stacks in Theorems
3.14
and
3.15
. From Theorem
1.5
, we prove that both
\mathscr{H}_{g}
and
\mathscr{R}_{g}
are closed substacks of
\mathscr{X}_{g}
and find a specific smooth open substack
\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}
containing
\mathscr{H}_{g}
and
\mathscr{R}_{g}
(see Definition-Proposition
4.9
). This allows us to prove:

Theorem 1.6

(see Theorem
4.12
)
.

The open substack
\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}
admits a good moduli space isomorphic to an open subscheme of the Chow quotient
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}_{g}
. The image of
\mathscr{H}_{g}\cup\mathscr{R}_{g}
is the unique Chow polystable point representing the doubled rational normal curve.

When
g=4
, we strengthen this result by proving
\mathscr{X}_{4}^{\circ}=\mathscr{X}_{4}
and the following.

Theorem 1.7

(see Theorem
5.1
)
.

For
g=4
, the stack
\mathscr{X}_{4}
admits a projective good moduli space isomorphic to the Chow quotient
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}_{4}
.

In Section
5
, this result is used to prove the existence of the hyperelliptic flip for
g=4
. The stack
\mathscr{X}_{4}
admits
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}_{4}\cong\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
as its good moduli space, and we define an open substack
\mathscr{X}_{4}^{+}\subset\mathscr{X}_{4}
which admits a good moduli space
\mathfrak{X}_{4}^{+}
with a proper morphism to
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
. This serves as the final local model of the Hassett–Keel program for
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9}+\epsilon)\to\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
in a neighborhood of the curve
\Gamma
. Furthermore, by defining a suitable local VGIT on
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}_{g}
, we expect that this construction yields the hyperelliptic flip in every genus (see Remark
5.15
).

2.
Preliminaries

Throughout this paper, we work over a fixed algebraically closed field
\mathbbm{k}
of characteristic zero.

2.1.
Stacks of curves

Following
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0D4Y
and
Tag 0DMJ
]
, let
\mathcal{C}urves
be the functor from
\mathbbm{k}
-schemes to groupoids defined as

\mathcal{C}urves(S):=\left\{f:X\to S\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\textrm{$X$ is an algebraic space, $f$ is a flat proper morphism}\\
\textrm{of finite presentation, and $f$ has relative dimension $\leq 1$}\end{array}\right.\right\}.

We note that our notation of
\mathcal{C}urves
refers to
\mathbbm{k}\textrm{-}\mathcal{C}urves
in
[
Sta25
]
. By
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0D5A
and
Tag 0DSS
]
(see also
[
dJHS11
,
Smy13
]
), we know that
\mathcal{C}urves
is a quasi-separated algebraic stack locally of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
.

A
curve
in this paper stands for a geometrically connected equidimensional scheme of dimension
1
and of finite type over some field extension
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
of
\mathbbm{k}
. Note that a
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
-point in
\mathcal{C}urves
may not parameterize a curve in our sense, although all relevant open substacks below will satisfy this.

Let
g
be a non-negative integer.
By
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0E1L
and
Tag 0E6K
]
, there is an open substack
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}
of
\mathcal{C}urves
such that
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}(S)
consists of
(f:X\to S)\in\mathcal{C}urves(S)
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

f
is Gorenstein whose fibers are equidimensional of dimension
1
;

(2)

f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{S}
, and this holds after arbitrary base change;

(3)

R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module of rank
g
.

In particular, a
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
-point
[C]\in\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}(\mathbbm{k}^{\prime})
for a field extension
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}/\mathbbm{k}
parameterizes a Gorenstein proper curve
C
over
\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
such that
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}^{\prime}
and
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}^{\prime g}
. In this case, we say that
g
is the
(arithmetic) genus
of
C
.
We also define two open substacks of
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}
as

\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}},

where
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}}
parameterizes curves in
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}
with local complete intersection singularities, and
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}
parameterizes curves in
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}}
whose singular locus is finite; see
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0E0J
and
Tag 0DZT
]
. Moreover, the algebraic stack
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}
is smooth by
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0DZT
]
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{g}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
denote open substacks of
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}
parameterizing smooth curves of genus
g
and Deligne–Mumford stable curves of genus
g
, respectively; see
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0E82
and
Tag 0E77
]
. Then we have open immersions

\mathcal{M}_{g}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{Gor}}.

By
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0E86
]
, we know that
\mathcal{M}_{g}
is open and dense in
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{g}
is irreducible by
[
DM69
]
, so are
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
and
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}
.

2.2.
Good moduli spaces for algebraic stacks

We recall the definition of good moduli spaces for algebraic stacks and their properties here.

Definition 2.1

(
[
Alp13
]
)
.

A quasi-compact and quasi-separated morphism
\pi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
between algebraic stacks
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
is a
good moduli space morphism
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}}\to\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
is an isomorphism;

(2)

\pi
is
cohomologically affine
, i.e.
\pi_{*}:\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathcal{Y})
is exact.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{Y}
is representable by an algebraic space
X
, then we say that
X
is a
good moduli space
of
\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
. Let
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbbm{k})
be a closed point. We say that
f:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
is a
local quotient presentation around
x
if:

(1)

the stabilizer
G_{x}
of
x
is linearly reductive;

(2)

there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{W}\cong\left[\Spec A/G_{x}\right]
, where
A
is a finite type
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra;

(3)

the morphism
f
is étale and affine; and

(4)

there exists a point
w\in\mathcal{W}(\mathbbm{k})
such that
f(w)=x
and
f
induces an isomorphism
G_{w}\cong G_{x}
.

The stack
\mathcal{X}
admits local quotient presentations if there exist local quotient presentations around all closed points
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbbm{k})
.

The following result guarantees the existence of local quotient presentations.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
AHR20
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type
over
\mathbbm{k}
with affine diagonal. If
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbbm{k})
is a closed point with linearly reductive stabilizer, then there exists a local quotient presentation
f:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
around
x
.

Moreover, we have the following criterion that guarantees the existence of a good moduli space.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
AFS17a
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
. Suppose the following conditions hold.

(1)

For every closed point
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbbm{k})
, there exists a local quotient presentation
f:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
around
x
such that
f
is stabilizer preserving at closed points of
\mathcal{W}
, and
f
sends closed points to closed points.

(2)

For every point
x\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbbm{k})
, the closed substack
\overline{\{x\}}
admits a good moduli space.

Then,
\mathcal{X}
admits a good moduli space.

Next, we introduce the concept of
\Theta
-reductivity and S-completeness which are closely related to the existence of good moduli spaces.

Definition 2.5

(
[
AHLH23
]
)
.

For a DVR
R
with a uniformizer
r
, we define two quotient stacks

\Theta_{R}:=[\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}:=[\Spec(R[s,t]/(st-r))/\mathbb{G}_{m}],

where the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{A}_{R}^{1}
is the standard multiplication action, and the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}
has weights
1,-1
in
s,t
. We denote by
0
the unique closed point in
\Theta_{R}
and
\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}
.

An algebraic stack
\mathcal{X}
is

(1)

\Theta
-reductive
if, for every DVR
R
, every morphism
\Theta_{R}\setminus 0\to\mathcal{X}
admits a unique extension
\Theta_{R}\to\mathcal{X}
;

(2)

S-complete
if, for every DVR
R
, every morphism
\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\setminus 0\to\mathcal{X}
admits a unique extension
\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\to\mathcal{X}
.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
AHLH23
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
with affine diagonal. Then
\mathcal{X}
admits a separated good moduli space
X
if and only if
\mathcal{X}
is
\Theta
-reductive and S-complete. Moreover, the good moduli space
X
is proper over
\mathbbm{k}
if and only if
\mathcal{X}
satisfies the existence part of the valuative criterion for properness.

Let us remark that it suffices to check the lifting criteria of Theorem
2.6
for DVRs that are essentially of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
; see
[
AHLH23
, Propositions 3.17, 3.41, Theorem 5.4, and Lemma A.11]
.

We collect some useful properties of good moduli spaces.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
Alp13
, Theorem 6.6]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
. Assume that
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to X
is a good moduli space for
\mathcal{X}
. Then,
\phi
is universal for maps from
\mathcal{X}
to algebraic spaces.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
Alp13
, Theorem 4.16(viii)]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
that admits a good moduli space
X
. If
\mathcal{X}
is reduced (resp. irreducible, normal), then so is
X
.

Definition 2.9

(
[
Alp13
, Definition 6.1 and Remark 6.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
that admits a good moduli space
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to X
. An open substack
\mathcal{U}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}
is
saturated
if
\mathcal{U}=\phi^{-1}(\phi(\mathcal{U}))
. If
\mathcal{U}
is saturated in
\mathcal{X}
, then
\phi(\mathcal{U})\subset X
is open and
\phi|_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{U}\to\phi(\mathcal{U})
is a good moduli space.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
Alp13
, Proposition 7.9]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
. Let
\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be open substacks of
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{X}=\cup_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{U}_{i}
and for each
1\leq i\leq m
, there exists a good moduli space
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{U}_{i}\to U_{i}
. Then there exists a good moduli space
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to X
and open sub-algebraic spaces
\widetilde{U}_{i}\subset X
such that
\widetilde{U}_{i}\cong U_{i}
and
\phi^{-1}(\widetilde{U}_{i})=\mathcal{U}_{i}
if and only if for each
1\leq i,j\leq m
, the intersection
\mathcal{U}_{i}\cap\mathcal{U}_{j}
is saturated in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

2.3.
Stacks from first three steps of Hassett–Keel program

We recall the notation

\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)=\Proj\big(\oplus_{m\geq 0}H^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g},\lfloor m(K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta)\rfloor)\big),

the log canonical model of
(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g},\alpha\delta)
, from the introduction. The Hassett–Keel program predicts that as
\alpha
decreases from one to zero there are certain “critical” values of
\alpha
where birational contractions occur to produce new modular compactifications of
\mathcal{M}_{g}
with increasingly worse singularities. In this section, we briefly review previously known results.

First, we recall that an
elliptic tail
is a connected genus
1
component of a stable curve meeting the rest of the curve at a single point. When
\alpha>\frac{7}{10}
and
\alpha\neq\frac{9}{11}
, the stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
is Deligne–Mumford for
g\geq 2
. Indeed:

•

When
\alpha>\frac{9}{11}
, there is an isomorphism
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)\cong\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}
(see e.g.
[
Has05
, Section 4]
or
[
HH09
, Section 1]
; the point is that
K_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}}+\alpha\delta
is ample provided
\frac{9}{11}<\alpha\leq 1
).

•

The first critical
\alpha
value occurs at
\alpha=\frac{9}{11}
. Indeed, by the work of
[
HH09
]
(
g\geq 4)
,
[
HL10
]
(
g=3
), and
[
Has05
]
(
g=2
), we have that
\overline{M}_{g}(\frac{9}{11})
is the coarse space of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}^{\rm ps}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}^{\rm ps}
denotes Schubert’s moduli stack of pseudo-stable curves. There is a divisorial contraction
\overline{M}_{g}\to\overline{M}_{g}(\frac{9}{11})
induced by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}^{\rm ps}
which corresponds to replacing elliptic tails with cusps. The curves parameterized by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}^{\rm ps}
have ample canonical sheaf, and at worst
A_{2}
-singularities not containing
A_{1}
-attached elliptic curves (i.e. elliptic tails). Pseudostable curves have finite automorphisms for
g>2
by
[
Sch91
, Proof of Lemma 5.3]
, and so the stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}^{\rm ps}
is Deligne–Mumford. Moreover, in the above mentioned works, the authors show that
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)\cong\overline{M}_{g}(\frac{9}{11})
whenever
\frac{7}{10}<\alpha\leq\frac{9}{11}
.

Before stating the next lemma, we summarize the curves
C
by parameterized by
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon,1)
. We use the notation
A_{\leq n}
to denote the class of
A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{n}
singularities. For the definition of
elliptic chains
and
Weierstrass chains
, we refer the reader to
[
AFSvdW17
, Definitions 2.3 & 2.4]
.

Definition 2.11
.

[
AFSvdW17
, Definition 2.5 & Theorem 2.7]

The stacks
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
parameterize proper reduced Gorenstein curves
C
of arithmetic genus
g\geq 4
with
\omega_{C}
ample satisfying the following constraints on their singularities and irreducible components depending on the value
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon,1]
.

•

For
\alpha\in(\frac{9}{11},1]
:
C
has only
A_{1}
singularities.

•

For
\alpha=\frac{9}{11}
:
C
has only
A_{1},A_{2}
singularities.

•

For
\alpha\in(\frac{7}{10},\frac{9}{11})
:
C
has only
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
singularities, and does not contain
A_{1}
-attached elliptic tails.

•

For
\alpha=\frac{7}{10}
:
C
has only
A_{\leq 3}
singularities, and does not contain
A_{1},A_{3}
-attached elliptic tails.

•

For
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3},\frac{7}{10})
:
C
has only
A_{\leq 3}
singularities, and does not contain:

–

A_{1},A_{3}
-attached elliptic tails,

–

A_{1}/A_{1}
-attached elliptic chains.

•

For
\alpha=\frac{2}{3}
:
C
has only
A_{\leq 4}
singularities, and does not contain:

–

A_{1},A_{3},A_{4}
-attached elliptic tails,

–

A_{1}/A_{1},A_{1}/A_{4},A_{4}/A_{4}
-attached elliptic chains.

•

For
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon,\frac{2}{3})
:
C
has only
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities, and does not contain:

–

A_{1},A_{3},A_{4}
-attached elliptic tails,

–

A_{1}/A_{1},A_{1}/A_{4},A_{4}/A_{4}
-attached elliptic chains, or

–

A_{1}
-attached Weierstrass chains.

Moreover, each
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
is an algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
with affine diagonal. Indeed, each
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
is an open substack of
\mathcal{C}urves_{g}^{\mathrm{lci}+}
.

The following summarizes the first three steps of the Hassett–Keel program in any genus
g\geq 2
. We note that there are minor differences when
g\in\{2,3\}
, so we state those cases separately.

Theorem 2.12

(Hassett–Keel program for
g\geq 4
;
[
HH09
,
HH13
,
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
,
AFS17b
]
)
.

For every
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon,1]
and every
g\geq 4
, the algebraic stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
admits a projective good moduli space isomorphic to
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
. Moreover, for each critical value
\alpha_{c}\in\{\frac{9}{11},\frac{7}{10},\frac{2}{3}\}
, we have a wall crossing diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha_{c}+\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha_{c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha_{c}-\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha_{c}+\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha_{c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha_{c}-\epsilon)}

where the top arrows are open immersions of algebraic stacks, the bottom arrows are projective morphisms, and the vertical arrows are good moduli space morphisms.

Theorem 2.13

(Hassett–Keel program for
g=2

[
Has05
,
HL07
]
and
g=3

[
HL10
]
)
.

Let
g\in\{2,3\}
.
For every
\alpha\in(\frac{7}{10},1]
, the algebraic stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
admits a projective coarse moduli space isomorphic to
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
. In particular:

•

if
\alpha\in(\frac{7}{10},\frac{9}{11}]
then
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
is the coarse space of the stack of pseudostable curves, and when
\alpha>\frac{9}{11}
, the space
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
is the coarse space of the Deligne–Mumford compactification.

•

When
g=2
, the space
\overline{M}_{2}(\alpha)
becomes a point for
\alpha=\frac{7}{10}
.

•

When
g=3
, the space
\overline{M}_{g}(\alpha)
admits a projective good moduli space for all
\alpha\in(\frac{5}{9},\frac{7}{10})
with explicit descriptions given in
[
HL10
]
. When
\alpha=\frac{5}{9}
, the space
\overline{M}_{3}(\alpha)
becomes a point.

The next lemma is special for
g=4
.

Lemma 2.14
.

When
\alpha\in(\frac{2}{3},\frac{7}{10})
, the stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
is a proper Deligne–Mumford stack.

Proof.

By Definition
2.11
, we know that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\alpha)
parameterizes curves with
A_{\leq 3}
-singularities, not containing
A_{1}
,
A_{3}
-attached elliptic tails or
A_{1}/A_{1}
-attached elliptic chains. Since
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
admits a proper good moduli space by
[
HH13
,
AFSvdW17
,
AFS17a
]
, it suffices to show that every closed point in the stack has finite stabilizer.

Assume to the contrary that there exists a closed point
[C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
with infinite stabilizer. By
[
HH13
, Corollary 8.8]
(see also
[
AFSvdW17
, Remark 2.28]
),
C
admits an
A_{1}/A_{1}
-attached rosary of length
3
. Since the rosary has arithmetic genus
2
, the remaining curve has to be either an
A_{1}/A_{1}
-attached elliptic bridge or two
A_{1}
-attached elliptic tails, which are not allowed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
. This is a contradiction. The proof is finished.
∎

2.4.
Chow stability and VGIT

2.4.1.
Chow stability

We first introduce the relevant Chow schemes and stacks for curves of genus
g\geq 3
.
Let
\bm{C}_{1,2g-2}(\mathbb{P}^{g-1})
be the Chow scheme of
\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
parameterizing
1
-cycles of degree
2g-2
. Denote by
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}
the seminormalization of the closure of the locus parameterizing the
1
-cycles of smooth canonical curves of genus
g
in
\bm{C}_{1,2g-2}(\mathbb{P}^{g-1})
.

Let
L_{\infty}
be the restriction of the Chow polarization on the Chow scheme
\bm{C}_{1,2g-2}(\mathbb{P}^{g-1})
to
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}
.

Definition 2.15
.

We say that a cycle in
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}
is
Chow (poly/semi)stable
if it is GIT (poly/semi)stable with respect to
L_{\infty}
.
Let
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}^{\rm ss}
be the open subscheme of
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}
parametrizing Chow semistable cycles. Then we have the Chow quotient stack

\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\rm c}:=[\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}^{\rm ss}/\mathrm{PGL}(g)].

with a good moduli space
\phi_{g}^{\rm c}:\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\rm c}\to\mathfrak{X}_{g}^{\rm c}=\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}^{\rm ss}\sslash\mathrm{PGL}(g)
.

Lemma 2.16
.

A smooth canonical curve of genus
g
in
\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
is Chow stable. A doubled rational normal curve in
\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
is Chow polystable.

Proof.

The first part follows from
[
BPTL16
, Corollary 2.8]
(see also
[
ACG+11
, Section XIV.3]
). The second part follows from the facts that the rational normal curve is always Chow polystable (see
[
Kem78
, Corollary 5.3]
) and any multiple of a Chow polystable cycle remains Chow polystable. Alternatively, one may deduce that a doubled rational normal curve in
\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
is Chow polystable using
[
Kem78
, Corollary 5.1]
together with the fact that the centralizer of the automorphism group of a doubled rational normal curve in
\mathrm{PGL}(g)
is trivial.
∎

2.4.2.
Chow stability and VGIT in genus
4

Let us specialize to the genus
g=4
case. For simplicity, we omit the subscript
4
from
\mathscr{X}_{4}^{\rm c}
,
\mathfrak{X}_{4}^{\rm c}
, etc. We also denote by
\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
(resp.
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm s}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
) the open substack (resp. open subscheme) parameterizing Chow stable cycles. From the theory of GIT we know that
\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
is a Deligne–Mumford stack with a coarse moduli space
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm s}
.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{9}=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{3},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(2)))
be the parameter space of quadric surfaces in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}\times\mathbb{P}^{9}
with second projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbb{P}^{9}
be the universal family of quadric surfaces. Let
E_{3}:=(\mathrm{pr}_{2})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Q}}(3,0)
be the vector bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{9}
whose fiber over
[Q]\in\mathbb{P}^{9}
is given by
H^{0}(Q,\mathcal{O}_{Q}(3))
. Let
\mathbb{P}(E_{3}):=\Proj_{\mathbb{P}^{9}}\mathrm{Sym}(E_{3}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbb{P}^{9}
be the projective bundle associated to
E_{3}
. Denote by

\eta:=\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{9}}(1)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\xi:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}(1).

Let
U_{(2,3)}\subset\mathbb{P}(E_{3})
be the open subscheme parameterizing
(2,3)
-complete intersections in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. We have a Hilbert–Chow morphism
\varphi_{(2,3)}:U_{(2,3)}\to\bm{C}_{1,6}(\mathbb{P}^{3})
. Since a general curve in
U_{(2,3)}
is a smooth canonical curve of genus
4
, as abuse of notation we have a factorization

\varphi_{(2,3)}:U_{(2,3)}\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}.

Let
W
denote the closure of the graph of
\varphi_{(2,3)}
in
\mathbb{P}(E_{3})\times\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
with reduced scheme structure. Denote by
p_{1}:W\to\mathbb{P}(E_{3})
and
p_{2}:W\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
the first and second projections respectively.

Definition 2.17
.

For each
t\in(0,\frac{2}{3})\cap\mathbb{Q}
, we choose
\delta\in(0,\min\{t,\frac{2}{9}\})\cap\mathbb{Q}
and define the VGIT quotient stack
\mathscr{X}_{t}
and space
\mathfrak{X}_{t}
of slope
t
as

\mathscr{X}_{t}:=[W^{\rm ss}(N_{t})/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathfrak{X}_{t}:=W^{\rm ss}(N_{t})\sslash\mathrm{PGL}(4),

where

N_{t}:=\tfrac{2-3t}{2-3\delta}p_{1}^{*}(\eta+\delta\xi)+\tfrac{t-\delta}{2-3\delta}p_{2}^{*}L_{\infty}.

These VGIT quotients are independent of the choice of
\delta
, see e.g.
[
CMJL14
,
LO21
,
ADL23
]
.

In
[
CMJL12
]
, the authors gave a complete description of cycles in
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
and
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
. To state their result, we first define the following curves.

Definition 2.18

(
[
CMJL12
]
)
.

Denote by
C_{2A_{5}}
the
(2,3)
complete intersection curve
V(x_{0}x_{3}-x_{1}x_{2},x_{0}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}x_{3})\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
, depicted in Figure
1
. On the quadric surface
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}=V(x_{0}x_{3}-x_{1}x_{2})
, this curve is the union of two sections of
\mathcal{O}(1,0)
, each meeting a section of
\mathcal{O}(1,3)
in an
A_{5}
-singularity. In particular,
C_{2A_{5}}
has two separating
A_{5}
-singularities.

Denote by
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{0}}\cup\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{3}}=V(x_{0}x_{3})\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
and by
C_{D}
the
(2,3)
complete intersection curve
V(x_{0}x_{3},x_{1}^{3}+x_{2}^{3})\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{0}}\cup\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{3}}
, depicted in Figure
1
. This curve is the union of two sets of three lines meeting at a point. In particular,
C_{D}
has two
D_{4}
-singularities and three
A_{1}
-singularities.

For
(A,B)\in\mathbbm{k}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
, denote by
C_{A,B}
the pencil of
(2,3)
complete intersection curves

V(x_{2}^{2}-x_{1}x_{3},Ax_{1}^{3}+Bx_{0}x_{1}x_{2}+x_{0}^{2}x_{3})\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}

contained in the singular quadric surface
V(x_{2}^{2}-x_{1}x_{3})
. If
4A/B^{2}\neq 0,1
, these curves have an
A_{5}
singularity at a smooth point of the quadric surface and an
A_{3}
singularity at the cone point. If
4A/B^{2}=0
, there is an additional
A_{1}
singularity at a smooth point of the quadric surface. If
4A/B^{2}=1
, this curve is a canonical ribbon of genus four (see section
3.2
).

(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},C_{2A_{5}})
A_{5}

(\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{0}}\cup\mathbb{P}^{2}_{x_{3}},C_{D})

C_{2A_{5}}
and

.

.

Theorem 2.19

(
[
CMJL12
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

The Chow stable locus
\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
precisely parameterizes curves
C\in|\mathcal{O}_{Q}(3)|
such that
Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a quadric surface with
\rank Q\geq 3
,
C
has at worst
A_{4}
-singularities at smooth points of
Q
, and at worst
A_{2}
-singularities at the vertex of
Q
if
\rank Q=3
.

The Chow strictly semistable locus
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
precisely parameterizes curves
C\in|\mathcal{O}_{Q}(3)|
such that
Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a quadric surface with
\rank Q\geq 2
, and, if
C
is reduced,
Q
is uniquely determined and
(Q,C)
satisfies:

(1)

if
\rank Q=4
, then either (a)
C
contains a singularity of type
D_{4}
or
A_{5}
, or (b)
C
does not contain an irreducible component of degree
\leq 2
and contains a singularity of type
A_{k}
,
k\geq 6
.

(2)

if
\rank Q=3
, then
C
has at worst an
A_{k}
singularity at the vertex of
Q
and either (a)
C
contains a
D_{4}
or
A_{5}
singularity at a smooth point of
Q
or an
A_{3}
singularity at the vertex of
Q
, or (b)
C
does not contain an irreducible component of degree
1
and contains a singularity of type
A_{k}
,
k\geq 6
at a smooth point of
Q
or of type
A_{k}
,
k\geq 4
at the vertex of
Q
.

(3)

if
\rank Q=2
, then
C
meets the singular locus of
Q
in 3 distinct points. These curves are called elliptic triboroughs.

If
C
is not reduced, then
C
is a genus
4
ribbon.

Moreover, the strictly Chow polystable locus
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\mathfrak{X}^{\rm s}
is precisely the union of two isolated points
C_{2A_{5}}
,
C_{D}
and a disjoint rational curve
\Gamma
parameterizing
C_{A,B}
(which contains a unique point representing the cycle of a ribbon).

Remark 2.20
.

From the description above, it is clear that any Chow stable curve
C
lies on a normal quadric surface
Q
such that
(Q,\frac{2}{3}C)
is klt. Furthermore, any Chow polystable point
C
other than the ribbon lies on a quadric surface such that
(Q,\frac{2}{3}C)
is slc.

Furthermore, it will follow from Proposition
5.7
that all Chow stable curves are contained in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
as in Definition
2.11
.

The following lemma was used implicitly in
[
CMJL12
]
. We provide a proof for the readers’ convenience.

Lemma 2.21
.

The Chow semistable open subscheme
\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}
is normal. In particular, the stack
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
and its good moduli space
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
are both normal.

Proof.

We look at the second projection map
p_{2}:W\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
. Let
W^{\rm ss}(L_{\infty}):=p_{2}^{-1}(\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss})
. Since
p_{2}
is birational and proper, we know that its restriction
W^{\rm ss}(L_{\infty})\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}
is birational and proper. By taking the Stein factorization, it suffices to show that
p_{2}
has connected fibers over any point
[C]\in\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}
.

We first assume that
[C]
is reduced. By
[
CMJL12
, Theorem 3.1]
there exists a unique quadric surface
Q=(q=0)
and a non-zero section
s\in H^{0}(Q,\mathcal{O}_{Q}(3))
unique up to scaling such that
C=V(q,s)
. We claim that
p_{2}^{-1}([C])
consists of only one point
([s],[C])
. Assume that
([s^{\prime}],[C])\in p^{-1}([C])
where
0\neq s^{\prime}\in H^{0}(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{Q^{\prime}}(3))
and
Q^{\prime}=V(q^{\prime})
is a quadric surface in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. From the construction we know that
C\subset V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})\subset Q^{\prime}
which implies that
Q^{\prime}=Q
is the unique quadric surface containing
C
. If
V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
is not a complete intersection, then under suitable coordinates we may write

q^{\prime}=x_{0}x_{1},\quad s=x_{0}\cdot f_{2}(x_{0},x_{2},x_{3}),

where
f_{2}
is a non-zero homogeneous polynomial of degree
2
. Thus
V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})\cap V(x_{1})
is a conic curve
V(x_{1},f_{2})
union the line
V(x_{0},x_{1})
. On the other hand, by
[
CMJL12
, Theorem 3.1(2)iii)]
we know that
C\cap V(x_{1})
is a reduced cubic curve not containing the line
V(x_{0},x_{1})
. Thus
C\cap V(x_{1})
is not contained in
V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})\cap V(x_{1})
which contradicts
C\subset V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
.
Thus
V(q^{\prime},s^{\prime})
is a complete intersection with the same degree as
C
which implies
[s^{\prime}]=[s]\in\mathbb{P}(E_{3})
.

Finally, we study the case that
[C]
is not reduced. Suppose
p_{2}([s],[C])=[C]
where
s\in H^{0}(Q,\mathcal{O}_{Q}(3))
is a non-zero section with
Q=V(q)
a quadric surface. Since
\Supp([C])
is a twisted cubic curve contained in
Q
, we know that
\rank(Q)\geq 3
which implies that
q
is irreducible. Hence
C=V(q,s)
is a complete intersection curve with
\phi(C)=[C]
. By
[
CMJL12
, Theorem 3.1(0)]
we know that
C
is a canonical genus
4
ribbon.
Since all canonical genus
4
ribbons are projectively equivalent by
[
BE95
]
, we know that
p_{2}^{-1}([C])
is a single orbit for the group
\Aut([C_{\mathrm{red}}])\cong\mathrm{PGL}(2)
which implies its connectedness.

The last statement follows from the fact that
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}=[\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]
and a good moduli space of a normal stack is normal (see Theorem
2.8
).
∎

Theorem 2.22

(
[
CMJL12
,
CMJL14
]
)
.

The Chow quotient
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
is isomorphic to
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
. Moreover, for every
\alpha\in(\frac{8}{17},\frac{5}{9})\cap\mathbb{Q}
and
t(\alpha):=\frac{34\alpha-16}{33\alpha-14}
, let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha):=\mathscr{X}_{t(\alpha)}
. Then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\alpha)
admits a projective good moduli space isomorphic to
\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha)
.

Remark 2.23
.

In Theorem
2.22
, we do not define
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
to be
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
. The key reason is that
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
does not parameterize curves but rather cycles. Later, in Sections
5
and
7
, we define
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})
as a new stack
\mathscr{X}
which admits a good moduli space isomorphic to
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
. We will see in Proposition
5.3
that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9}-\epsilon)
admits an open immersion to the new stack
\mathscr{X}
.

In Section
7
, we will describe the wall crossings for
\overline{M}_{4}(\alpha)
as
\alpha
ranges in
[\frac{5}{9},\frac{2}{3})
. For completeness, we describe below the last wall crossing from
[
CMJL14
]
as
\alpha
increases from
\frac{5}{9}-\epsilon
to
\frac{5}{9}
.

Theorem 2.24

(
[
CMJL14
, Theorem 6.3]
)
.

All curves parameterized by the final VGIT quotient stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44}):=\mathscr{X}_{t\in(\frac{6}{11},\frac{2}{3})}
are reduced
(2,3)
complete intersection curves in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. The VGIT (semi-)stable points
[(C)]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
are precisely the Chow (semi-)stable (see Theorem
2.19
) that are not ribbons, elliptic triboroughs, or curves on a quadric cone with a tacnode (an
A_{3}
singularity) at the vertex of the cone.

The next result is a summary of
[
CMJL14
, Theorem 3.1, Theorem 6.3]
.

Theorem 2.25

(
[
CMJL14
]
)
.

The morphism
\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9}-\epsilon)\to\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})\cong\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
is an isomorphism above
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}-\{[C_{D}]\cup\Gamma\}
. The preimage of
[C_{D}]
parameterizes unions of three conics meeting in two
D_{4}
singularities. The preimage of a generic point of
\Gamma
consists of reduced curves
C
on normal quadrics satisfying all of the following conditions:

•

C
has at worst a cusp at a singular point of the quadric (including the case of not passing through a singular point);

•

C
has an
A_{5}
singularity at a smooth point of the quadric;

•

any component of
C
passing through the
A_{5}
singularity has degree at least
2
.
1
1

1

For genus reasons, a curve on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
satisfying these conditions cannot contain a line. Curves on the smooth quadric containing a line through the
A_{5}
singular point are precisely those with non-separating
A_{5}
singularities and specialize to the point
[C_{2A_{5}}]
in
\mathscr{X}^{\mathrm{c}}
.

The preimage of the point in
\Gamma
corresponding to the ribbon parameterizes reduced curves
C
on normal quadrics satisfying all of the following conditions:

•

C
has at worst a cusp at a singular point of the quadric (including the case of not passing through a singular point);

•

C
has an
A_{6},A_{7},A_{8},
or
A_{9}
singularity at a smooth point of the quadric;

•

if
C
lies on a smooth quadric, then
C
does not contain an irreducible component of degree
\leq 2
; and

•

if
C
lies on a quadric cone, then
C
does not contain an irreducible component of degree
1
.

2.5.
Moduli of pairs and log Calabi–Yau wall crossing

In this subsection, we adapt the the moduli theory of boundary polarized Calabi–Yau pairs developed in
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
to study the moduli of genus four curves. We also introduce the relevant K-moduli and KSBA moduli spaces and setup the log Calabi–Yau wall crossing.

Definition 2.26
.

A
boundary polarized CY pair
is a projective slc pair
(X,cD)
such that

(1)

D
is an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier Weil divisor on
X
, and
c\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
;

(2)

K_{X}+cD\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}0
.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
c\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
satisfying
Nc\in\mathbb{Z}
.
A
family of boundary polarized CY pairs

(X,cD)\to B
over a Noetherian scheme
B
with coefficients
c
and index dividing
N
consists of
a flat projective morphism
X\to B
and a relative K-flat Mumford divisor
D
,
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

(X_{b},cD_{b})
is a boundary polarized CY pair for each
b\in B
;

(2)

\omega_{X/B}^{[N]}(NcD)\cong_{B}\mathcal{O}_{X}
;

(3)

\omega_{X/B}^{[m]}(nD)
commutes with base change for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 2.28

(
[
ABB+23
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
c\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
satisfying
Nc\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\chi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Z}
be a function.
There exists an algebraic stack
\mathcal{M}(\chi,N,c)
locally of finite type with affine diagonal that represents the moduli functor that send a scheme
B
over
\mathbbm{k}
to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\textrm{families of boundary polarized CY pairs }(X,cD)\to B\textrm{ with coefficient $c$ and }\\
\textrm{index dividing $N$ satisfying }\chi(X_{b},\omega_{X_{b}}^{[-m]})=\chi(m)\textrm{ for any $b\in B$ and $m\in\mathbb{N}$.}\end{array}\right\}

We are interested in the case of compactifications of moduli spaces of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}D)
where
D\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(3,3)|
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
\chi(m)=\chi(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}})=(2m+1)^{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{4}^{\circ}
denote the open substack of
\mathcal{M}(\chi,3,\frac{2}{3})
whose
\mathbbm{k}
-points are pairs
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}D)
where
D
is a smooth curve of bidegree
(3,3)
.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
denote the stack theoretic closure of
\mathcal{M}_{4}^{\circ}
in
\mathcal{M}(\chi,3,\frac{2}{3})
. We call
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
the
boundary polarized Calabi–Yau moduli stack
for genus four curves.

Theorem 2.30

(
[
ABB+23
, Theorem 16.11]
and
[
BL24
, Theorem 7.5]
)
.

The algebraic stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is irreducible, of finite type with affine diagonal. Moreover,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is S-complete,
\Theta
-reductive, satisfies the existence part of valuative criterion for properness, and admits a good moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
as an irreducible projective scheme.

We will see in Theorem
6.16
that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is smooth, and that its good moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is a normal projective variety isomorphic to the Baily–Borel compactification of Kondō
[
Kon02
]
of non-hyperelliptic genus four curves.

Definition 2.31
.

We define the
K-moduli stack

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
and the
KSBA moduli stack

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
for genus four curves as follows.

(1)

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
be the substack of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
consisting of families
(X,\frac{2}{3}D)\to B
such that for each
b\in B
,
(X_{b},(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)D_{b})
is a K-semistable log Fano pair for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
.

(2)

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
be the substack of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
consisting of families
(X,\frac{2}{3}D)\to B
such that for each
b\in B
,
(X_{b},(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)D_{b})
is slc for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
.

Before stating the log Calabi–Yau wall crossing theorem, we recall some deformation theory results from
[
ABB+23
]
.

Proposition 2.32
.

If
[(X_{0},\frac{2}{3}D_{0})]
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
and the normalization
(\overline{X}_{0},\overline{G}_{0})
of
(X_{0},0)
is plt, then
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is smooth at
[(X_{0},\frac{2}{3}D_{0})]
.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
[
ABB+23
, Proposition 11.5]
, using
[
HP10
, Proposition 3.1]
and
[
ACC+16
, Lemma 6]
(in addition to
[
Hac04
, Theorems 8.2, 9.1]
as used in loc. cit.) to obtain the canonical covering family.
∎

Theorem 2.33

(
[
ABB+23
,
BL24
]
)
.

Both
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
are open substacks of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
. Furthermore, the following hold.

(1)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
is an algebraic stack of finite type with affine diagonal. Moreover, it admits a good moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
as a normal, irreducible projective scheme.

(2)

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
is a separated Deligne–Mumford stack of finite type. Moreover, it admits a coarse moduli space
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
as an irreducible projective scheme.

(3)

There is a commutative wall crossing diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.72 -25.97 L 14.88 -25.97}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{-1.0}{10.55063pt}{-18.77196pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-1.0 0.0 0.0 -1.0 14.6 -25.97)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the top arrows are open immersions, the vertical arrows are good moduli space morphisms, and the bottom arrows are projective birational morphisms.

Proof.

This follows from
[
BL24
, Theorems 1.1 and 7.5]
(see also
[
ABB+23
, Theorem 16.11]
). The normality of
\overline{M}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
follows from
[
ADL23
, Theorem 2.21]
.
∎

The locus of klt pairs
(X,\frac{2}{3}D)
is contained in each of the three stacks.

Proposition 2.34
.

If
[(X,\frac{2}{3}D)]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is a klt pair, then
[(X,\frac{2}{3}D)]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
and
[(X,\frac{2}{3}D)]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
. In fact,
(X,(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)D)
is K-stable for all
0<\epsilon\ll 1
.

Proof.

The KSBA stability is straightforward: because
(X,\frac{2}{3}D)
is klt,
(X,(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)D)
is lc for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
and hence
[(X,\frac{2}{3}D)]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{KSBA}}
. The K-semistability follows from
[
Zho23
, Lemma 5.3]
because there are no lc places for the klt pair, and by
[
ABB+23
, Proposition 6.13, Theorem 6.15]
, the pairs are in fact K-stable. ∎

2.6.
Curves on quadric cones

In this subsection, we collect some useful results on curves of degree
6
in
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
C
be a curve on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
of degree
6
with an
A_{4}
-singularity at the cone vertex
[0,0,1]
. Then
C
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to the strictly GIT polystable hyperelliptic curve
(x_{2}^{2}=x_{0}^{5}x_{1}^{5})\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,5)_{[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]}
(see Definition
3.6
).

Proof.

Let
[x,y,z]
be the projective coordinates of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.
Denote by
C=(f(x,y)=0)
in the affine coordinate chart
z=1
.
Since
C
has an
A_{4}
-singularity at the cone vertex, we know that the covering curve
\widetilde{C}=(f(x,y)=0)\subset\mathbbm{k}^{2}_{(x,y)}
has an
A_{7}
-singularity at the origin. Thus after change of coordinates we may assume that the tangent line at the origin is
(y=0)
, and there exists analytic coordinates
(x,y^{\prime})
such that

(2.1)

f(x,y)=y^{\prime 2}+ax^{8}+\textrm{higher order terms},

where
(x,y^{\prime})
has weight
(1,4)
and
a\neq 0
. Moreover, since the tangent line is
(y=0)
, we may choose
y^{\prime}
to be a
4
-jet of the form
y^{\prime}=y-ux^{3}
since it is semi-invariant under the
\bm{\mu}_{2}
-action
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,-y)
. If
u=0
then we must have
a=0
which is contradiction to the assumption of an
A_{4}
singularity. Thus we may assume
u=1
after a rescaling of the coordinates. Then, we may write

(2.2)

f(x,y)=y^{2}-2x^{3}y+g_{6}(x,y)=y^{\prime 2}+(g_{6}(x,y^{\prime}+x^{3})-x^{6}),

where
g_{6}(x,y)=\sum_{i=0}^{6}b_{i}x^{i}y^{6-i}
is a degree
6
homogeneous polynomial in
(x,y)
. Combining (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), we get
b_{6}=1
and
b_{5}=a\neq 0
. Again after a rescaling of
(x,y)
with weight
(1,3)
, we may assume that
b_{5}=-1
. Thus we can write

f(x,y)=y^{2}-2x^{3}y+x^{6}-x^{5}y+y^{2}h_{4}(x,y),

where
h_{4}(x,y)=\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}x^{i}y^{4-i}
is a degree
4
homogeneous polynomial in
(x,y)
.

Next we construct the test configuration degenerating
C
to the given hyperelliptic curve. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle=V(sz_{3}-z_{1}^{3}+z_{0}z_{2})\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)_{[z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}]}\times\mathbb{A}_{s}^{1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=V(z_{3}^{2}-z_{0}^{2}z_{1}^{2}z_{2}-sz_{0}z_{1}^{2}z_{3}+\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}s^{10-2i}z_{0}^{6-i}z_{1}^{i})|_{\mathcal{X}}

We consider the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
by

t\cdot([z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}],s)=([z_{0},t^{2}z_{1},t^{6}z_{2},t^{5}z_{3}],ts).

It is straightforward to check that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{C})
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant which makes it a test configuration.

Finally, we analyze the fibers of
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
. When
s=1
, we can identify
\mathcal{X}_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)_{[x,y,z]}
under the closed immersion
\iota:\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)
where
\iota([x,y,z])=[y,x,z,x^{3}-yz]
. Then the equation of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
in
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
becomes

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(x^{3}-yz)^{2}-x^{2}y^{2}z-x^{2}y(x^{3}-yz)+\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}x^{i}y^{6-i}

\displaystyle=y^{2}z^{2}-2x^{3}yz+x^{6}-x^{5}y+y^{2}h_{4}(x,y).

Thus in the affine coordinate
z=1
we get precisely the equation
f(x,y)=0
, which implies that
(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})\cong(\mathbb{P}(1,1,2),C)
.

When
s=0
, we can identify
\mathcal{X}_{0}
with
\mathbb{P}(1,2,9)_{[y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}]}
under the closed immersion
\iota^{\prime}:\mathbb{P}(1,2,9)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)
where
\iota^{\prime}([y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}])=[y_{0}^{3},y_{0}y_{1},y_{1}^{3},y_{2}]
. Then the equation of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
in
\mathbb{P}(1,2,9)
becomes
0=y_{2}^{2}-y_{0}^{8}y_{1}^{5}
. We claim that
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is isomorphic to the strictly GIT polystable hyperelliptic curve. Indeed, we can take another closed immersion
\iota^{\prime\prime}:\mathbb{P}(1,2,9)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}(1,1,5,9)_{[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]}
where
\iota^{\prime\prime}([y_{0},y_{1},y_{2}])=[y_{0}^{2},y_{1},y_{0}y_{2},y_{2}^{2}]
. Then
(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})
is identified with
(V(x_{2}^{2}-x_{0}x_{3}),V(x_{2}^{2}-x_{0}x_{3},x_{3}-x_{0}^{4}x_{1}^{5}))
inside
\mathbb{P}(1,1,5,9)
. Thus
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is isomorphic to
V(x_{2}^{2}-x_{0}^{5}x_{1}^{5})
inside
\mathbb{P}(1,1,5)_{[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]}
after substituting
x_{3}
by
x_{0}^{4}x_{1}^{5}
. The proof is finished.
∎

Proposition 2.36
.

Let
C
be a curve on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)_{[x,y,z]}
of degree
6
with an
A_{3}
-singularity at the cone vertex
[0,0,1]
. Then after change of coordinates we may write

(2.3)

C=(y^{2}z^{2}+Bx^{3}yz+Ax^{6}+y^{2}h_{4}(x,y)=0)

where
4A\neq B^{2}
. Moreover,
C
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to
C_{A,B}
. If in addition
h_{4}\neq 0
, then
C
has at worst
A_{4}
-singularities in the smooth locus of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.

Proof.

Let
[x,y,z]
be the projective coordinates of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
.
Denote by
C=(f(x,y)=0)
in the affine coordinate chart
z=1
.
Since
C
has an
A_{3}
-singularity at the cone vertex, we know that the covering curve
\widetilde{C}=(f(x,y)=0)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
has an
A_{5}
-singularity at the origin. Thus after change of coordinates we may assume that the tangent line at the origin is
(y=0)
, and there exists an analytic coordinate
(x,y^{\prime})
such that

f(x,y)=y^{\prime 2}+ax^{6}+\textrm{higher order terms},

where
(x,y^{\prime}=y-ux^{3})
has weight
(1,3)
and
a\neq 0
. Thus we may write

(2.4)

f(x,y)=y^{2}+Bx^{3}y+Ax^{6}+\textrm{higher order terms},

where
(x,y)
has weight
(1,3)
and
(A,B)=(a+u^{2},-2u)
satisfies
4A-B^{2}=4a\neq 0
.

Next we claim that there exists a unique unipotent automorphism
\sigma:[x,y,z]\mapsto[x+vy,y,z+q(x,y)]
that transforms
C
into the standard form (
2.3
) where
v\in\mathbbm{k}
and
q(x,y)
is a degree
2
homogeneous polynomial. According to (
2.4
) we have
C=(F(x,y,z)=0)
where

F(x,y,z)=y^{2}z^{2}+g_{4}(x,y)z+g_{6}(x,y),

where
g_{4}(x,y)=Bx^{3}y+\sum_{i=0}^{2}a_{i}x^{i}y^{4-i}
and
g_{6}(x,y)=Ax^{6}+\sum_{i=0}^{5}b_{i}x^{i}y^{6-i}
. Thus
\sigma^{*}C=(F\circ\sigma(x,y,z))=0)
where

\displaystyle F\circ\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=y^{2}(z+q)^{2}+g_{4}(x+vy,y)(z+q)+g_{6}(x+vy,y)

\displaystyle=y^{2}z^{2}+(2y^{2}q+g_{4}(x+vy,y))z+(y^{2}q^{2}+g_{4}(x+vy,y)q+g_{6}(x+vy,y))

\displaystyle=:y^{2}z^{2}+g_{4}^{\sigma}(x,y)z+g_{6}^{\sigma}(x,y).

For every
v\in\mathbbm{k}
there exists a unique
q=\frac{Bx^{3}y-g_{4}(x+vy,y)}{2y^{2}}
such that
g_{4}^{\sigma}=Bx^{3}y
. Then we have

\displaystyle g_{6}^{\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=g_{6}(x+vy,y)+Bx^{3}yq-y^{2}q^{2}

\displaystyle=Ax^{6}+(b_{5}+6Av+B\cdot\mathrm{coeff}_{x^{2}}(q))x^{5}y+\textrm{lower order terms}

From the above description we have
\mathrm{coeff}_{x^{2}}(q)=-\frac{1}{2}(a_{2}+3Bv)
. Thus we have

\mathrm{coeff}_{x^{5}y}(g_{6}^{\sigma})=b_{5}+6Av-\frac{B}{2}(a_{2}+3Bv)=b_{5}-\frac{Ba_{2}}{2}+\frac{3}{2}(4A-B^{2})v.

Since
4A-B^{2}\neq 0
, there exists a unique
v\in\mathbbm{k}
such that
\mathrm{coeff}_{x^{5}y}(g_{6}^{\sigma})=0
. As a result, we have

F\circ\sigma(x,y,z)=y^{2}z^{2}+Bx^{3}yz+Ax^{6}+y^{2}h_{4}(x,y),

where
h_{4}(x,y):=\frac{g_{6}^{\sigma}-Ax^{6}}{y^{2}}
,
and the claim is proved.

Next we show the statement on degenerations. Suppose
C
has the standard form (
2.3
). Let
\rho:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\Aut(\mathbb{P}(1,1,2))
be a
1
-PS such that
\rho(t)\cdot[x,y,z]=[x,t^{-1}y,tz]
. Then clearly
\rho(t)_{*}C=(y^{2}z^{2}+Bx^{3}yz+Ax^{6}+t^{2}y^{2}h_{4}(x,ty)=0)
which implies that
\lim_{t\to 0}\rho(t)_{*}C=C_{A,B}
.

Finally we address the singularities of
C
in the smooth locus of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
. Assume that
h_{4}\neq 0
. We first look at the affine chart
y=1
, and consider the new coordinates
(x,z^{\prime}=z+\frac{B}{2}x)
. Then
C
is defined by the equation

z^{\prime 2}+\frac{4A-B^{2}}{4}x^{6}+\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}x^{i}=0.

Since
4A-B^{2}\neq 0
and
h_{4}\neq 0
, we know that the polynomial
\frac{4A-B^{2}}{4}x^{6}+\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}x^{i}
has no root of multiplicity
6
. Thus
C
has at worst
A_{4}
-singularities in the affine chart
y=1
. Next, we look at the affine chart
x=1
, where
C
is defined by the equation

y^{2}z^{2}+Byz+A+\sum_{i=0}^{4}b_{i}y^{6-i}=0.

We only need to look at the singularities on the line
(y=0)
, which is non-empty if and only if
A=0
. By analyzing the partial derivatives in
y
and
z
, we conclude that
C
has at worst an
A_{1}
-singularity at
[1,0,0]
, and is smooth elsewhere on the line
y=0
and
x\neq 0
. Thus the proof is finished.
∎

3.
Hyperelliptic curves and ribbons

In this section, we revisit the moduli theory of hyperelliptic curves and ribbons of genus
g\geq 2
after
[
AV04
,
BE95
]
. We define moduli stacks
\mathscr{H}_{g}
of hyperelliptic curves and
\mathscr{R}_{g}
of ribbons (Definition
3.12
) and give explicit quotient stack presentations (Theorems
3.14
and
3.15
). We also show that the deformation of the hyperelliptic ribbon (the unique curve in
\mathscr{H}_{g}\cap\mathscr{R}_{g}
) is unobstructed (Theorem
3.17
).

3.1.
Hyperelliptic curves

Definition 3.1
.

A
hyperelliptic curve
of (arithmetic) genus
g
is a proper curve
C
over
\mathbbm{k}
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}
and
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}^{g}
;

(2)

there exists a finite morphism
\varphi:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
is a sheaf of rank two.

Definition 3.2
.

A
Weierstrass curve
of (arithmetic) genus
g
is a Cartier divisor

C
of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)_{[x,y,z]}
defined by the equation
C=(z^{2}=f(x,y))
where
f
is a (possibly zero) binary form of degree
2g+2
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Every hyperelliptic curve is isomorphic to a Weierstrass curve. Conversely, every Weierstrass curve is hyperelliptic. In particular, if
C
is a hyperelliptic curve, then
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
is a locally free sheaf of rank
2
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

We start by showing the first statement.
Let
C
be a hyperelliptic curve of genus
g
. Since
\varphi
is surjective,
\varphi^{\#}:\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
is non-zero at the generic point of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Then
H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}
implies that
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
is torsion free as an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-module. Hence
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
is locally free of rank
2
. By the Grothendieck splitting theorem, we have
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(a)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(b)
for some integers
a\geq b
. Then
h^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C})=1
and
h^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C})=g
implies that
a=0
and
b=-g-1
. Denote by
L:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-g-1)
so we have
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L
. Moreover, we may choose the isomorphism so that
\varphi^{\#}(s)=(s,0)
. Then we have
C\cong\Spec_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

Next, we analyze the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L
. It is clear that the algebra structure is determined by a
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-module homomorphism
L^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L
, which then corresponds to a section
(h_{2},h_{1})\in H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L^{\otimes-2}\oplus L^{\otimes-1})
. We claim that
C
is isomorphic to the Cartier divisor
C^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
defined by
(z^{2}-h_{1}(x,y)z-h_{2}(x,y)=0)
. Indeed, let
S:=\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)\setminus\{[0,0,1]\}
. Then we have the projection map
\varphi_{S}:S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined by
\varphi_{S}([x,y,z])=[x,y]
. Moreover,
\varphi_{S}
realizes
S
as the total space of the line bundle
L^{\vee}
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so we have

S\cong\Spec_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus_{m=0}^{\infty}L^{\otimes m}.

Since
(z=0)
corresponds to the zero section of
\varphi_{S}
, we have that
z
corresponds to a nowhere vanishing section
z_{S}\in H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g+1))
, such that

C^{\prime}\cong\Spec_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus_{m=0}^{\infty}L^{\otimes m}/(z_{S}^{2}-h_{1}(x,y)z_{S}-h_{2}(x,y)).

Since
z_{S}^{m}
generates
L^{\otimes m}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m(g+1))
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we know that

C^{\prime}\cong\Spec_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L,

where the algebra structure is determined by

L^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L,\quad s\mapsto(s\otimes h_{2},s\otimes h_{1})\quad\textrm{for any local section $s$ of $L$}.

Thus we have
C\cong C^{\prime}
. After a change of coordinate
[x,y,z]\mapsto[x,y,z-\frac{h_{1}}{2}]
, we see that
C^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the curve
C^{\prime\prime}:=(z^{2}=f(x,y))
in
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
where
f=\frac{h_{1}^{2}}{4}+h_{2}
. Thus we have that
C\cong C^{\prime\prime}
, and
\varphi
corresponds to
\varphi_{S}|_{C^{\prime\prime}}
under this isomorphism.

Conversely, we shall show that a Weierstrass curve is always hyperelliptic. If
C=(z^{2}=f(x,y))
is a Weierstrass curve in
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
, then the above argument implies that
\varphi=\varphi_{S}|_{C}:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is affine and
\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L
, and hence
H^{i}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\varphi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{C})
. This implies that
\varphi
is finite and hence
C
is hyperelliptic. The proof is finished.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
C
be a hyperelliptic curve of genus
g
. Then
C
has local complete intersection singularities,
|\omega_{C}|
is base point free and induces a map
f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
. Moreover,
f
can be factored into
f=\iota\circ\varphi
where
\iota:\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
is a closed embedding whose image is a rational normal curve. In particular,
\varphi
is unique up to automorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.3
, we may assume that
C
is a Weierstrass curve in
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
. Since
C
is a Cartier divisor in the smooth surface
S=\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)\setminus\{[0,0,1]\}
, we know that
C
has local complete intersection singularities. By adjunction, we have
\omega_{C}\cong\omega_{S}(C)|_{C}
. Moreover,

\omega_{S}(C)\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)}(K_{\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)}+C)|_{S}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)}(g-1)|_{S}\cong\varphi_{S}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1).

Thus we have
\omega_{C}\cong\varphi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1)
which implies that
|\omega_{C}|
is base point free. Moreover, we have

H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})\cong H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\varphi_{*}\omega_{C})\cong H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1)\oplus(L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1)))\cong H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1)).

Here we used the fact that
L=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-g-1)
which implies
H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1))=0
. Hence
f
factors into
f=\iota\circ\varphi
where
\iota:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
is induced by the complete linear system
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(g-1)|
. The last statement on
\varphi
follows easily from this as it is determined by the canonical map
f
up to projective equivalence.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
C,C^{\prime}
be hyperelliptic curves of genus
g
. Then,
C\cong C^{\prime}
if and only if
C
and
C^{\prime}
are equivalent as Weierstrass curves, i.e. by an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
.

Proof.

The backwards implication is clear. For the forward implication, suppose there is an isomorphism
\alpha:C\to C^{\prime}
and denote the map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
by
\varphi
and
C^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
by
\varphi^{\prime}
. By Theorem
3.3
, we may assume each curve is a Weierstrass curve with
C
given by
(z^{2}=f(x,y))
and
C^{\prime}
by
(z^{2}=f^{\prime}(x,y))
.
If both
C
and
C^{\prime}
are non-reduced, then we must have
f=f^{\prime}=0
and hence the Weierstrass curves are the same. Let us assume that both
C
and
C^{\prime}
are reduced, i.e. both
f
and
f^{\prime}
are non-zero.
By Proposition
3.4
,
\varphi^{\prime}\circ\alpha
is a hyperelliptic map
C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so
\varphi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\beta\circ\varphi
where
\beta
is an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Therefore, the ramification divisors of
C
and
C^{\prime}
must coincide via
\beta
, but the ramification divisors are precisely
(f(x,y)=0)
and
(f^{\prime}(x,y)=0)\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Therefore, taking a suitable lifting of
\beta\in\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{1})
as an element of
\Aut(\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
acting on the first two coordinates, we see that
C
and
C^{\prime}
differ by an automorphism of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

A Weierstrass curve
C=(z^{2}=f(x,y))\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
is called
GIT (poly/semi)stable
if the binary form
f
is non-zero and GIT (poly/semi)stable. A hyperelliptic curve is called
GIT (poly/semi)stable
if its isomorphic Weierstrass curve is GIT (poly/semi)stable. By Corollary
3.5
, this is independent of the choice of the isomorphic Weierstrass curve. We denote by
\mathfrak{H}_{g}^{+}
the GIT moduli space of hyperelliptic curves of genus
g
.

3.2.
Ribbons

We briefly recall the concept of ribbons from
[
BE95
]
.

Definition 3.7
.

A
(rational) ribbon
is a scheme
C
of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
equipped with an isomorphism
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbbm{k}}\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{\mathrm{red}}
, such that the nilradical sheaf
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{O}_{C}
satisfies
\mathcal{N}^{2}=0
, and that
\mathcal{N}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{C_{\mathrm{red}}}
-module of rank
1
. We often denote by
L
the sheaf
\mathcal{N}
considered as an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-module, and call it the
conormal bundle
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
in
C
.

We say that a ribbon
C
has
(arithmetic) genus

g
if
\chi(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})=1-g
. By Riemann–Roch, this is equivalent to saying that
L\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-g-1)
. In particular, we have
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}
and
H^{1}(C,\mathcal{O}_{C})\cong\mathbbm{k}^{g}
.

A ribbon
C
is called
split
if the morphism
C_{\mathrm{red}}\to C
admits a retraction
C\to C_{\mathrm{red}}
. By
[
BE95
, Proposition 1]
there exists a unique split ribbon of each genus.

Example 3.8
.

Let
R_{\mathrm{hyp}}:=(z^{2}=0)\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,g+1)
be a specific hyperelliptic curve. Then there is a finite morphism
\varphi:R_{\mathrm{hyp}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
given by
\varphi([x,y,z])=[x,y]
. Moreover,
R_{\mathrm{hyp}}\cong\Spec_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus L
with
L=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-g-1)
, where the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-algebra structure satisfies
L^{2}=0
. Thus
R_{\mathrm{hyp}}
is a split ribbon of genus
g
. We call
R_{\mathrm{hyp}}
the
hyperelliptic ribbon of genus
g
. Since split ribbons are unique, we know that every split ribbon is isomorphic to the hyperelliptic ribbon.

For any ribbon
C
with conormal bundle of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
denoted
L
as above, there is a short exact sequence

0\to L\to\Omega_{C}|_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to 0

from restriction of
\Omega_{C}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By the standard interpretation of Ext groups (see, for example
[
Har77
, Exercise III.6.1]
),
\Omega_{C}|_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
corresponds to an element of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}},L)
, denoted by
e_{C}
. This extension class classifies the ribbon as given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.9

(
[
BE95
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Given any class
e\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}},L)
, there is a unique ribbon
C
with
e=e_{C}
. Moreover, two ribbons
C
and
C^{\prime}
are isomorphic over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
if and only if
e_{C}=ae_{C^{\prime}}
for some
a\in\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
. In particular, a ribbon
C
is split if and only if
e_{C}=0
.

Proposition 3.10

(
[
BE95
, Theorem 5.3]
)
.

Let
C
be a ribbon of genus
g
. Then
|\omega_{C}|
is base point free and induces a map
f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{g-1}
