f:[\Spec(A)/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to\mathscr{Y}_{g}
around
y
. Denote by
[V(I)/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
the preimage of
\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{g}\cup\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}_{g}
under
f
. Then from the local description we know that the stabilizer is trivial in
\Spec(A)\setminus V(I)
after possibly shrinking
\Spec(A)
. Moreover, since
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\rib}
is a good moduli space morphism by Lemma
4.13
, using
[
AHLH23
, Proposition 4.3]
we know that after possibly shrinking
\Spec(A)
, the restriction
[V(I)/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{g}\cup\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}_{g}
of
f
satisfies condition (1). Thus condition (1) holds at closed points
y\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{g}\cup\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}_{g}
.

Next, we check condition (2) of Theorem
2.4
. If
y\in\mathscr{Y}_{g}\setminus(\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{g}\cup\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}_{g})
, then
y
is a closed point with trivial stabilizer, thus (2) holds. If
y\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{g}\cup\widetilde{\mathscr{R}}_{g}
, then by Lemma
4.13
and
[
ABHLX20
, Remark 2.11]
we know that (2) holds. Thus by Theorem
2.4
we conclude that
\mathscr{Y}_{g}
admits a good moduli space
\mathfrak{Y}_{g}
.

Finally we show that
\mathfrak{Y}_{g}\cong V_{g,1}
. By universality of good moduli spaces (see Theorem
2.7
), we have a morphism
g:\mathfrak{Y}_{g}\to V_{g,1}
. From the above arguments and Lemma
4.13
, we know that
g
is bijective and
\mathfrak{Y}_{g}
is normal. Thus
g
is separated. By Zariski’s main theorem of algebraic spaces
[
Sta25
,
Tag 082K
]
, we conclude that
g
is an isomorphism.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.12
.

By Proposition
4.14
, we have that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}:\mathscr{Y}_{g}\to V_{g,1}
is cohomologically affine, and
(\widetilde{\Phi}_{g})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Y}_{g}}=\mathcal{O}_{V_{g,1}}
. This implies that
\Phi_{g}^{\circ}:\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}\to\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}
is cohomologically affine and
(\Phi_{g}^{\circ})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}}
as
\widetilde{\Phi}_{g}
is a
\mathrm{PGL}(g)
-torsor over
\Phi_{g}^{\circ}
. Thus
\Phi_{g}^{\circ}
is a good moduli space morphism. Since
\phi_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}:\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}
is a good moduli space morphism, we conclude that the composition
\phi_{g}^{\circ}:\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{X}_{g}^{\rm c,\circ}
is a good moduli space morphism. This proves the theorem.
∎

5.
The hyperelliptic flip

5.1.
Good moduli spaces for genus
4
curves with Chow semistable cycles

From now on, we shall focus on the case of genus
g=4
. For simplicity, we omit the subscript
4
from
\mathscr{X}_{4}
,
\mathscr{X}_{4}^{\rm c}
,
\mathfrak{X}_{4}^{\rm c}
, etc. to denote the corresponding stack or space by
\mathscr{X}
,
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
,
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
, etc. In particular,
\mathscr{X}
parameterizes Gorenstein smoothable curves of genus
4
with ample and basepoint free canonical bundle whose cycle-theoretic canonical image is Chow semistable.

The following is the main result of this subsection which strengthens Theorem
4.12
in genus
4
.

Theorem 5.1
.

The stack
\mathscr{X}
is a smooth irreducible algebraic stack of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
with affine diagonal that contains
\mathcal{M}_{4}
as a dense open substack. We have an open immersion
\mathscr{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}urves_{4}^{\mathrm{lci}}\cap\mathcal{C}urves_{4,1}
.
Moreover,
\mathscr{X}
admits a projective good moduli space isomorphic to
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}=\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{X}^{{\rm c},\circ}=\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
.

Proof.

We only need to show the second equality as the first one follows by Definition-Proposition
4.9
. Since
\mathscr{X}^{{\rm c},\circ}
is the largest saturated open substack of
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
contained in
[U_{4,1}/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]
, it suffices to show that
U_{4,1}=\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}
. By Theorem
2.19
we know that every
1
-cycle in
\mathbf{Chow}_{g,1}^{\rm ss}
is either a reduced
(2,3)
-complete intersection curve in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
or a doubled rational normal curve. Thus it follows from Proposition
4.8
that
U_{4,1}=\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}
and the proof is finished.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

By Lemma
5.2
we can identify
\mathscr{X}
with
\mathscr{X}^{\circ}
. Thus the first statement follows from
Theorems
4.3
and
4.11
. The second statement follows from Definition-Proposition
4.9
. The last statement follows from Theorem
4.12
.
∎

5.2.
The hyperelliptic flip in genus
4

In this subsection, we verify that
\mathscr{X}
contains
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
as an open substack and we introduce
an open substack
\mathscr{X}^{+}
of
\mathscr{X}
and show that it admits a proper good moduli space which replaces ribbons by hyperelliptic curves in genus
4
. If we let
\mathscr{X}^{-}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}
be a suitable open substack that provides a model for the Hassett–Keel wall crossing near
\Gamma
(see Remark
5.14
), this will give the wall crossing
\mathscr{X}^{-}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}\hookleftarrow\mathscr{X}^{+}
that provides the flip over the locus
\Gamma
of
C_{A,B}
curves, illustrated in Figure
3
.

Proposition 5.3
.

There is an open immersion
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
is the last VGIT quotient stack from Theorem
2.24
. Consequently, there is a diagram of stacks and good moduli spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\cong\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9})}

where the bottom arrow is a projective morphism.

Proof.

We use notation as in Section
2.4
. Denote by
\mathscr{X}_{t}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
the VGIT quotient stack from
[
CMJL12
,
CMJL14
]
for
t\in(\frac{6}{11},\frac{2}{3})
(see also Theorem
2.22
). By Definition
2.17
,
\mathscr{X}_{t}=[W^{\rm{ss}}(N_{t})/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]
, where
W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))\subset W
. By Theorems
2.19
and
2.24
, every point of
W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))
represents a reduced
(2,3)
complete intersection curve lying on a uniquely determined quadric. By
[
Kol96
, Corollary I.6.6.1]
, the Hilbert–Chow morphism is an isomorphism along this locus, and thus the induced morphism
W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))\hookrightarrow W\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
is birational and quasi-finite. Furthermore, the locus
\mathbf{Chow}^{\rm{ss}}_{4,1}\subset\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
is open, and by Theorem
2.24
, the image of
W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))\to\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}
is contained in
\mathbf{Chow}^{\rm{ss}}_{4,1}
.

Therefore, by Zariski’s Main Theorem and Lemma
2.21
,
W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))\to\mathbf{Chow}^{\rm{ss}}_{4,1}
is an open immersion.
This yields an open immersion of stacks
\mathscr{X}_{t}=[W^{\rm{ss}}(N(t))/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]\to\mathscr{X}^{\rm{c}}=[\mathbf{Chow}^{\rm{ss}}_{4,1}/\mathrm{PGL}(4)]
such that the image does not contain the point representing a doubled rational normal curve. Therefore, by Theorem
4.11
(3) and Lemma
5.2
, there is an open immersion
\mathscr{X}_{t}\to\mathscr{X}
, as desired.

The last sentence follows from Theorem
5.1
and universality of the good moduli space morphism
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})\to\overline{M}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
among maps to algebraic spaces (see Theorem
2.7
).
∎

Definition 5.4
.

Recall that
\Phi:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
is the Hilbert–Chow morphism, and

\phi=\phi^{\rm c}\circ\Phi:\mathscr{X}\to\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}=\mathbf{Chow}_{4,1}^{\rm ss}\sslash\mathrm{PGL}(4)

is a good moduli space morphism by Theorem
5.1
.
We denote by
\mathscr{U}:=\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\{[C_{2A_{5}}],[C_{D}]\})
and
\mathscr{V}:=\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\Gamma)
two open substacks of
\mathscr{X}
. Then we have
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{U}\cup\mathscr{V}
. Moreover,
\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{V}=\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}^{\rm s})=\Phi^{-1}(\mathscr{X}^{\rm s})
is isomorphic to the Chow stable stack
\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
by Theorem
4.11
and Lemma
5.2
. By abuse of notation, we simply write
\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\mathscr{X}^{+}
be the substack of
\mathscr{X}
parametrizing curves
C
satisfying either of the following conditions.

(1)

C
has
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities;

(2)

C
belongs to
\mathscr{V}
, i.e. there are no special degenerations of the
1
-cycle
\Phi(C)
to
[C_{A,B}]
for any
(A,B)\in\mathbbm{k}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
.

By deformation theory of
A_{k}
-singularities, we know that condition (1) defines an open substack of
\mathscr{X}
. By Definition
5.4
we know that
\mathscr{V}
is an open substack
\mathscr{X}
. Thus
\mathscr{X}^{+}
is an open substack of
\mathscr{X}
. We denote by
\mathscr{U}^{+}:=\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{X}^{+}
.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Theorem 5.6
.

Notation as above. The stack
\mathscr{X}^{+}
admits a good moduli space
\mathfrak{X}^{+}
as a proper algebraic space.

In order to prove Theorem
5.6
, we first focus on the open substack
\mathscr{U}^{+}
of
\mathscr{X}^{+}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

The stack
\mathscr{U}^{+}
precisely parameterizes curves
C
in
\mathscr{U}
with at worst
A_{4}
-singularities. Moreover,
\mathscr{U}^{+}
is an open substack of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
.

Proof.

For the first statement, notice that a curve in
\mathscr{U}
with
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities belongs to
\mathscr{X}^{+}
and hence
\mathscr{U}^{+}
by Definition
5.5
. Conversely, a curve
[C]
in
\mathscr{U}^{+}
satisfies that either
C
has
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities, or
[C]\in\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{V}
. Since
\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
which only contains curves with
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities by Theorem
2.19
, we know that
[C]
always has
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities.

Next, we shall focus on the second statement. From Theorem
5.1
, Definitions
2.11
and
5.5
we know that both
\mathscr{U}^{+}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
are open substacks of
\mathcal{C}urves_{4}^{\mathrm{lci}}
. Thus it suffices to show that
\mathscr{U}^{+}(\mathbbm{k})\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)(\mathbbm{k})
. We have shown that every curve
C\in\mathscr{U}^{+}(\mathbbm{k})
has
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities. Thus we only need to show that the three cases of elliptic tails, elliptic chains and Weierstrass chains do not appear in
\mathscr{U}^{+}
. Since
\mathscr{U}^{+}
is open, it suffices to show that these three cases do not appear when each of the elliptic tail/elliptic chain/Weierstrass chain is smooth.

(1)

elliptic tails
(E,p)
:

•

A_{1}
-attached. Then
\omega_{C}|_{E}\cong\omega_{E}(p)\cong\mathcal{O}_{E}(p)
is not base point free, a contradiction.

•

A_{2}
-attached. Then
\omega_{C}|_{E}\cong\omega_{E}(2p)\cong\mathcal{O}_{E}(2p)
is base point free but not very ample. Thus the canonical image of
C
is non-reduced and contains a line as the image of
E
. This is a contradiction.

•

A_{4}
-attached. Then
E
is the normalization of
C
, and
p_{a}(C)=p_{a}(E)+2=3\neq 4
, a contradiction.

(2)

elliptic chains
(E,p_{1},p_{2})
:

•

length is at least
3
. Then
p_{a}(C)\geq 3+2=5>4
, a contradiction.

•

length is
2
. If
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
is
A_{4}
-attached, then
p_{a}(C)\geq 2+1+2=5>4
, a contradiction.

•

length is
1
. If both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are
A_{4}
-attached, then
p_{a}(C)\geq 1+2+2=5>4
, a contradiction.
If
p_{1}
is
A_{1}
-attached and
p_{2}
is
A_{4}
-attached, then
p_{a}
of the image of
E
in
C
is
3
, which implies that the remaining curve is an
A_{1}
-attached elliptic tail, a contradiction.
If both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are
A_{1}
-attached, then
\omega_{C}|_{E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{E}(p_{1}+p_{2})
. Thus the canonical image of
C
is non-reduced and contains a line as the image of
E
, a contradiction.

(3)

Weierstrass chain
(E,p)
:

•

length is at least
2
. Let
(E^{\prime},p^{\prime})
be the genus
2
component with Weierstrass point. Then
\omega_{C}|_{E^{\prime}}\cong\omega_{E^{\prime}}(2p^{\prime})\cong\pi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)
where
\pi:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the double cover map. Hence
\pi_{*}\omega_{C}|_{E^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(2)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)
which implies that
\omega_{C}|_{E^{\prime}}
is base point free but not very ample. Thus the canonical image of
C
is non-reduced and contains a conic as the image of
E^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

•

length is
1
. Then
\omega_{C}|_{E}\cong\omega_{E}(p)
is not base point free, a contradiction.

∎

Proposition 5.8
.

The closed points
[C]
of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
are one of the following.

(1)

C
is a GIT polystable hyperelliptic curve;

(2)

C\in\mathscr{X}\setminus\mathscr{H}
is a canonical curve on a normal quadric
Q
with at worst
A_{3}
-singularities at a singularity of
Q
and at worst
A_{4}
-singularities elsewhere.

Moreover, every
\mathbbm{k}
-point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to a closed point.

Proof.

We first prove that (1) and (2) are both closed points.

For (1), let
\mathscr{H}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{H}
be the open substack parameterizing GIT semistable hyperelliptic curves. From the GIT of binary forms we know that
\mathscr{H}^{+}
precisely parameterizes hyperelliptic curves with
A_{\leq 4}
-singularities. Thus we have
\mathscr{H}^{+}=\mathscr{H}\cap\mathscr{U}^{+}
. By Proposition
4.5
we know that
\mathscr{H}^{+}
is a closed substack of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
. Thus every GIT polystable hyperelliptic curve being a closed point of
\mathscr{H}^{+}
by GIT is also a closed point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
.

For (2), by Theorem
2.19
we know that if
C
is a canonical curve with at worst
A_{2}
-singularities at singularity of
Q
and at worst
A_{4}
-singularities elsewhere, then
[C]\in\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
. Thus
C
is a closed point of
\mathscr{X}
and hence of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
.

Next we assume that
C
has an
A_{3}
-singularity at the singularity of
Q
, and at worst
A_{4}
-singularities elsewhere. By
[
CMJL12
, Proof of Theorem 3.1]
we know that
C
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to
C_{A,B}
with
4A\neq B^{2}
which has an
A_{3}
-singularity at the singularity of
Q
and an
A_{5}
-singularity in the smooth locus of
Q
(and another
A_{1}
-singularity if
B=0
). Since
\Aut^{0}(C_{A,B})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we know that this is the only other point in the closure of
[C]
in
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
. Since
[C_{A,B}]\notin\mathscr{U}^{+}
, we know that
[C]
is a closed point.

Next we show that every closed point
[C]
of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
is either (1) or (2). The hyperelliptic case follows from GIT. In the canonical case, we have that
Q
is normal as otherwise
C
degenerates to
C_{D}
which implies
[C]\not\in\mathscr{U}
, a contradiction. Assume to the contrary that
C
has an
A_{4}
-singularity at the singularity of
Q
. Then Proposition
2.35
implies that
C
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to the unique hyperelliptic curve with two
A_{4}
-singularities, i.e.
(x_{2}^{2}=x_{0}^{5}y_{1}^{5})\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,5)_{[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]}
, which is a closed point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
from case (1), a contradiction.

Finally, the last statement follows from the discussion above.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Every closed point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
is also closed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Let
[C]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
be a closed point. If
C
has at worst
A_{3}
-singularities, then by Definition
2.11
we know that
[C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
. Thus Lemma
2.14
implies that
[C]
is a closed point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
. Since
[C]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
, it is also a closed point in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
by local VGIT (see
[
AFSvdW17
, Section 3]
and
[
AFS17a
, Theorem 1.3]
).

From now on, we may assume that
C
has at least an
A_{4}
-singularity by Proposition
5.8
. Let
[C_{0}]
(resp.
[C_{0}^{\prime}]
) be the unique closed point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3})
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
) in the closure of
[C]
. By
[
AFSvdW17
, Theorem 2.22]
, we know that
C_{0}=K\cup E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup E_{r}
, such that each
E_{i}
is an
\alpha_{\frac{2}{3}}
-atom and
[K]
is a closed point in the stack of
(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
-stable (pointed) curves with no
A_{1}
-attached Weierstrass tails. Since every
\alpha_{\frac{2}{3}}
-atom has arithmetic genus
2
, we know that
r\leq 2
. Below we split to three cases based on the value of
r
.

If
r=0
, then
[C_{0}]=[K]
belongs to the stack
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
which implies that
C_{0}
has at worst
A_{3}
-singularities. This is a contradiction to the assumption that
C
has at least an
A_{4}
-singularity.

If
r=1
, then
C_{0}=K\cup E_{1}
where
[(K,q)]
is a closed point in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2,1}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
with
q=K\cap E_{1}
. If moreover
\Aut(K,q)
is infinite, then by
[
AFSvdW17
, Remark 2.28]
we know that
K=K_{0}\cup R_{1}
where
R_{1}
is an
A_{1}/A_{1}
-attached rosary of length
3
. Then we must have
p_{a}(K_{0})=0
and
K_{0}
is a one pointed curve, which is a contradiction to the ampleness of
\omega_{K}(q)|_{K_{0}}
. Thus
\Aut(K,q)
must be finite which implies that
\Aut^{0}(C_{0})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Since
C_{0}
admits an
A_{1}
-attached Weierstrass tail, we know that
C_{0}\not\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. Thus
C_{0}^{\prime}
admits a non-trivial
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to
C_{0}
which implies that
\Aut(C_{0}^{\prime})
is finite. Hence
C\cong C_{0}^{\prime}
is closed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
.

If
r=2
, then
C_{0}=E_{1}\cup E_{2}
and
\Aut(C_{0})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}\rtimes\bm{\mu}_{2}
. Similar to the
r=1
case, we have
C_{0}\not\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. Thus we have
\dim\Aut(C_{0}^{\prime})<\dim\Aut(C_{0})=2
. If moreover
\Aut(C_{0}^{\prime})
is finite, then
C\cong C_{0}^{\prime}
is closed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. Hence we may assume
\Aut^{0}(C_{0}^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Denote by
q=E_{1}\cap E_{2}
. By
[
AFSvdW17
, Lemma 3.18 and Proposition 3.20, Type C]
, we have

\mathrm{T}^{1}(C_{0})=\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{1})\oplus\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{2})\oplus\mathrm{T}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{0},q})\cong\mathbbm{k}^{5}\oplus\mathbbm{k}^{5}\oplus\mathbbm{k}.

Denote by
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
the two standard
1
-PS’s of
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}
, i.e.
\sigma_{1}(t)=(t,1)
and
\sigma_{2}(t)=(1,t)
. Then from loc. cit. we know that
\sigma_{i}
acts on
\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{i})
with weights
(1,-10,-8,-6,-4)
, on
\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{3-i})
trivially, and on
\mathrm{T}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{0},q})
with weight
1
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. In addition, the
\bm{\mu}_{2}
-action swaps
\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{1})
and
\mathrm{T}^{1}(E_{2})
and acts trivially on
\mathrm{T}^{1}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{0},q})
. Thus by analyzing the
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}\rtimes\bm{\mu}_{2}
orbits, we see that
C_{0}^{\prime}
is either of the form
K_{i}\cup E_{3-i}
, or an irreducible curve with two
A_{4}
-singularities. The former is not possible as it contains a
A_{1}
-attached Weierstrass tail which implies
[C_{0}^{\prime}]\not\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. The latter is precisely the strictly GIT polystable hyperelliptic curve
(z^{2}=x^{5}y^{5})\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,5)
which is a closed point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
by Proposition
5.8
. Thus
C
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to
C_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{U}^{+}
and both points are closed. This implies
C\cong C_{0}^{\prime}
is closed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. The proof is finished.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

In the
r=1
case of the previous proof, it is shown that any closed point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2,1}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
has finite stabilizer. In particular, this implies that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{2,1}(\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon)
is a DM stack.

Corollary 5.11
.

\mathscr{U}^{+}
is a saturated open substack of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. In particular,
\mathscr{U}^{+}
admits a separated good moduli space.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
[C_{1}]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
and
[C_{2}]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
admit a common
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to a closed point
[C_{0}]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
, then
[C_{2}]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
. By Proposition
5.8
we know that
[C_{1}]
admits a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant degeneration to a closed point
[C_{0}^{\prime}]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
. By Proposition
5.9
, we know that
[C_{0}^{\prime}]
is also closed in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon)
. Thus
[C_{0}]=[C_{0}^{\prime}]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
. By openness of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
from Proposition
5.7
we know that
[C_{2}]\in\mathscr{U}^{+}
as well. The last statement follows from
[
Alp13
, Remark 6.2]
. The proof is finished.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.6
.

We first show that
\mathscr{X}^{+}
admits a good moduli space. By Theorem
2.19
, a curve
[C]\in\mathscr{V}
either belongs to the Chow stable locus
\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
(hence has at worst
A_{4}
-singularities), or degenerates to
[C_{2A_{5}}]
or
[C_{D}]
. Thus
\mathscr{V}
is an open substack of
\mathscr{X}^{+}
. By Corollary
5.11

\mathscr{U}^{+}
admits a separated good moduli space which we denote by
\mathfrak{U}^{+}
. By definition we know that
\mathscr{V}\cong\Phi(\mathscr{V})=(\phi^{\rm c})^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\Gamma)
admits a separated good moduli space
\mathfrak{V}\cong\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\Gamma
.

Next we show that
\mathscr{U}^{+}\cap\mathscr{V}
is saturated in both
\mathscr{U}^{+}
and
\mathscr{V}
. Since
\mathscr{V}\subset\mathscr{X}^{+}
, we know that
\mathscr{U}^{+}\cap\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{X}^{+}\cap\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{X}^{\rm s}
. In particular, every
\mathbbm{k}
-point of
\mathscr{U}^{+}\cap\mathscr{V}
is closed with finite inertia in
\mathscr{X}
. Thus
\mathscr{U}^{+}\cap\mathscr{V}
is saturated in both
\mathscr{U}^{+}
and
\mathscr{V}
. Thus Theorem
2.10
implies that
\mathscr{X}^{+}=\mathscr{U}^{+}\cup\mathscr{V}
admits a good moduli space which we denote by
\mathfrak{X}^{+}=\mathfrak{U}^{+}\cup\mathfrak{V}
.

Next we show that the good moduli space
\mathfrak{X}^{+}
of
\mathscr{X}^{+}
is separated. By
[
AHLH23
, Theorem 5.4]
, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{X}^{+}
is S-complete with respect to DVRs
(R,\mathfrak{m},\kappa)
that are essentially of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
. Let
f:\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\setminus 0\to\mathscr{X}^{+}
be a map. Then
f
is obtained by gluing two maps
f_{1},f_{2}:\Spec R\to\mathscr{X}^{+}
such that
f_{1}|_{\Spec K}=f_{2}|_{\Spec K}
where
K=\mathrm{Frac}(R)
. If the image of
f
lies in
\mathscr{U}^{+}
(resp. in
\mathscr{V}
), then there exists an extension
\bar{f}:\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\to\mathscr{U}^{+}
(resp.
\bar{f}:\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\to\mathscr{V}
) by the S-completeness of
\mathscr{U}^{+}
(resp. of
\mathscr{V}
). Moreover, such an extension is unique by
[
ABHLX20
, §3.2]
(see also
[
ABB+23
, §5.2]
). If the image of
f
is not contained in
\mathscr{U}^{+}
or
\mathscr{V}
, after possibly switching
t
and
s
we may assume that
f_{1}([\mathfrak{m}])\in\mathscr{U}^{+}\setminus\mathscr{V}
and
f_{2}([\mathfrak{m}])\in\mathscr{V}\setminus\mathscr{U}^{+}
. This implies that
\phi(f_{1}([\mathfrak{m}]))\in\Gamma
and
\phi(f_{2}([\mathfrak{m}]))\in\{C_{2A_{5}},C_{D}\}
. By the S-completeness of
\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
, we know that
\Phi\circ f:\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}\setminus 0\to\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
admits an extension to
\overline{\mathrm{ST}}_{R}
. In particular, we have
\phi(f_{1}([\mathfrak{m}]))=\phi(f_{2}([\mathfrak{m}]))
. This contradicts the fact that
\Gamma
and
\{C_{2A_{5}},C_{D}\}
are disjoint in
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
. Thus the separatedness of
\mathfrak{X}^{+}
is proved.

Finally, we show that
\mathfrak{X}^{+}
is proper. By Theorem
2.7
, the composition
\mathscr{X}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{X}^{\rm c}
induces a morphism
\psi:\mathfrak{X}^{+}\to\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
between separated good moduli spaces. By Lemma
2.21
and Theorem
5.1
we know that both
\mathfrak{X}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
are normal and irreducible.
Moreover, from the construction we know that
\psi
is birational. Since
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
is normal, irreducible and proper, by Lemma
5.12
it suffices to show that every fiber of
\psi
over
\mathbbm{k}
-points is proper and non-empty. Since
\mathscr{V}
is saturated in
\mathscr{X}^{+}
, we know that
\psi
is an isomorphism over
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}\setminus\Gamma
. Thus it suffices to show that
\psi^{-1}([C_{A,B}])
is proper and non-empty for every
(A,B)\in\mathbbm{k}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
. By Proposition
5.8
we know that
\psi^{-1}(\Gamma)
precisely consists of closed points of the stack
\mathscr{U}^{+}\setminus\mathscr{V}
, which are either GIT polystable hyperelliptic curves, or curves of degree
6
on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
with an
A_{3}
-singularity at the cone vertex and at worst
A_{4}
-singularities elsewhere.

If
4A=B^{2}
, then
C_{A,B}
is the canonical ribbon which we denote by
C_{R}
. By Proposition
2.35
,

\psi^{-1}([C_{R}])
precisely consists of GIT polystable hyperelliptic curves. Thus we have an isomorphism
\mathfrak{H}^{+}\cong\psi^{-1}([C_{R}])
which implies the properness of
\psi^{-1}([C_{R}])
, where
\mathfrak{H}^{+}=\mathfrak{H}_{4}^{+}
is as in Definition
3.6
.

If
4A\neq B^{2}
, then by Proposition
2.36
,
\psi^{-1}([C_{A,B}])
precisely consists curves of the form
(y^{2}z^{2}+Bx^{3}yz+Ax^{6}+h_{4}(x,y)=0)
where
h_{4}\neq 0
. Such curves are parameterized by the weighted projective stack
[\mathbb{A}^{5}\setminus\{0\}/\mathbb{G}_{m}]
where the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
\mathbb{A}^{5}\cong H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{[x,y]},\mathcal{O}(4))
with weight
(1,3)
on
(x,y)
. Thus we have a morphism

[(\mathbb{A}^{5}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}]\to\mathscr{X}^{+}

that descends to a morphism
(\mathbb{A}^{5}\setminus\{0\})\sslash\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathfrak{X}^{+}
on good moduli spaces whose image equals
\psi^{-1}([C_{A,B}])
. Thus the properness of
\psi^{-1}([C_{A,B}])
follows from the properness of
(\mathbb{A}^{5}\setminus\{0\})\sslash\mathbb{G}_{m}
. The proof is finished.
∎

The following lemma was used in the previous proof.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a birational morphism between normal irreducible separated algebraic spaces of finite type over
\mathbbm{k}
.
Assume that
f
is surjective. If every fiber of
f
over
\mathbbm{k}
-points of
Y
is proper, then
f
is proper.

Proof.

By the Nagata compactification for algebraic spaces
[
Rao74
,
CLO12
]
, there exists an open immersion
j:X\to\overline{X}
to an algebraic space
\overline{X}
and a proper morphism
\bar{f}:\overline{X}\to Y
such that
f=\bar{f}\circ j
. By replacing
\overline{X}
with the normalization of the irreducible component containing
X
, we may assume that
\overline{X}
is normal irreducible as well. By Stein factorization theorem for algebraic spaces
[
Sta25
,
Tag 0AYI
]
, we know that
\bar{f}^{-1}(y)
is connected for every
y\in Y(\mathbbm{k})
. Since
f^{-1}(y)
is proper, non-empty, and open in
\bar{f}^{-1}(y)
, we have
f^{-1}(y)=\bar{f}^{-1}(y)
for every
y\in Y(\mathbbm{k})
. Thus we have
X(\mathbbm{k})=\overline{X}(\mathbbm{k})
which implies that
X=\overline{X}
. Thus
f=\bar{f}
is proper.
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

The open immersion
\mathscr{U}^{+}\to\mathscr{U}
descends to a proper birational morphism of good moduli spaces
\mathfrak{U}^{+}\to\mathfrak{U}
with exceptional locus consisting of the curves with
A_{3}
singularities at the singularity of
Q
mapping to the associated point of
C_{A,B}
in Proposition
2.36
and GIT polystable hyperelliptic curves mapping to
C_{R}
, the canonical ribbon.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of Theorem
5.6
.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

Analogously to
\mathscr{X}^{+}
, we can define a companion open substack
\mathscr{X}^{-}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}
of
(2,3)
complete intersection curves
C=V(f,q)
in
\mathscr{X}
where, if
Q=V(q)
is a singular quadric surface,
C
has at worst
A_{2}
singularities at the vertex of
Q
. Moreover, one can show that there is a wall crossing diagram as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}^{+}}

Here the top arrows are open immersions of stacks, the bottom arrows are birational morphisms between proper varieties, and the the vertical arrows are good moduli space morphisms. One can show that
\mathscr{X}^{-}
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}(\frac{5}{9},\frac{23}{44})
are isomorphic near the locus of curves whose Chow polystable degeneration lies in
\Gamma
.

Combining Proposition
5.3
and the diagram from Remark
5.14
, we illustrate the wall crossing for
\alpha=\frac{5}{9}
near the curve
\Gamma\subset\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
in Figure

. The description of the preimage on the left side follows from Theorem
2.25
and the description of the preimage on the right from Corollary
5.13
. For a complete description of the wall crossing at
\alpha=\frac{5}{9}
, see Theorem
7.12
.

Remark 5.15
.

We expect the construction of
\mathscr{X}^{\pm}
and the wall crossing diagram from Remark
5.14
can be generalized to any genus as open substacks
\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ,\pm}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}
that admit separated good moduli spaces
\mathfrak{X}_{g}^{\circ,\pm}\to\mathfrak{X}_{g}^{\circ}
fitting into a similar diagram. This would provide a local model for the hyperelliptic flip in every genus
g\geq 4
. The idea is to follow the local VGIT approach of
[
AFS17a
, Theorem 1.3]
at the hyperbolic ribbon
[R_{\mathrm{hyp}}]
. By Proposition
4.6
and
[
AHR20
]
, we know that the étale local structure of
\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}
at
[R_{\mathrm{hyp}}]
is given by

[\mathbb{A}(T_{\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ},[R_{\mathrm{hyp}}]})/\Aut(R_{\mathrm{hyp}})]\cong[\mathbb{A}(H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L^{\otimes-2})\oplus\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}},L))/(\mathrm{GL}(2)/\bm{\mu}_{g+1})],

where
L=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-g-1)
. The center
\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}/\bm{\mu}_{g+1}
of
\mathrm{GL}(2)/\bm{\mu}_{g+1}
acts on
H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L^{\otimes-2})
and
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}},L)
with weights
2
and
-1
respectively. Thus we can have a VGIT wall crossing for the
\mathrm{GL}(2)/\bm{\mu}_{g+1}
-action on
\mathbb{A}(H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},L^{\otimes-2})\oplus\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{1}},L))
via the non-trivial character
\det:\mathrm{GL}(2)\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
in the sense of
[
Tha96
,
DH98
]
.
We expect that this VGIT on the étale Luna slice can be extended to give
\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ,\pm}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{g}^{\circ}
.

6.
Log Calabi–Yau wall crossing

In this section, we describe the wall crossing for curves with separating
A_{5}
-singularities or
D_{4}
-singularities. Both loci can be replaced using the log Calabi–Yau wall crossing framework developed in
[
ABB+23
]
. Our approach is to glue the good moduli spaces therein with the Chow quotient.
Note that these loci from the Chow moduli space are described in
[
CMJL12
, Section 3]
(see also
[
CMJL14
, Remark 3.3]
).

6.1.
Curves with separating
A_{5}
-singularities

In the Chow moduli space
\mathfrak{X}^{\rm c}
, there is a unique polystable point with separating
A_{5}
-singularities:
C_{2A_{5}}=V(x_{0}x_{3}-x_{1}x_{2},x_{0}x_{2}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}x_{3})
. In the associated wall crossing in the Hassett–Keel program, this is replaced by curves on a surface that is two copies of a
(1,3)
-weighted blow-up of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
, glued them along the ruling with weight
1
upside down. The exceptional locus after the wall is a divisor as the birational transform of
\delta_{2}
. As we shall see later, this locus is replaced at
\alpha=\frac{19}{29}
, which matches the prediction from
[
CMJL14
]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The pair
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}})
, depicted in Figure
1
, is K-polystable in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
.

Proof.

By
[
Fed12
, Section 2.1]
,
C_{2A_{5}}
is GIT polystable in the GIT moduli stack of
(3,3)
curves on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By
[
ADL23
, Theorem 1.4]
,
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\epsilon C_{2A_{5}})
is K-polystable for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
. Because
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}})
is log canonical log Calabi–Yau, it is K-semistable, and hence by interpolation
[
ADL24
, Proposition 2.13]
,
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},cC_{2A_{5}})
is K-polystable for all
c\in(0,\frac{2}{3})
. In particular, it is K-polystable for
c=\frac{2}{3}-\epsilon
so polystable in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{K}}
.
∎

In
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
, however, the pair
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}})
does not remain polystable. It admits a further degeneration to a curve on the non-normal surface
S_{2A_{5}}
defined below.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\pi:S_{A_{5}}\to\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
be the weighted blow-up of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)_{[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]}
at the point
p_{1}=[1,0,0]
with weight
(1,3)
in
(x_{1},x_{2})
, depicted in Figure
4
. Denote by
\ell_{1}\subset S_{A_{5}}
the strict transform of
(x_{1}=0)\subset\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
. Let
[u_{0},u_{1}]
be the coordinates of
\ell_{1}
such that
\pi([u_{0},u_{1}])=[u_{0},0,u_{1}^{3}]
. Let
S_{2A_{5}}
be the surface obtained as the union of two isomorphic surfaces
S_{A_{5}}
and
S_{A_{5}}^{\prime}
glued along
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{1}^{\prime}
via the map
[u_{0},u_{1}]\mapsto[u_{0}^{\prime},u_{1}^{\prime}]:=[u_{1},u_{0}]
, depicted in Figure
5
.

S_{A_{5}}
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
\pi^{-1}(p_{1})
l_{1}

\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
p_{1}
(x_{1}=0)

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{weighted blow-up of }p_{1}}

Figure 4.
The construction of
S_{A_{5}}
.

\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
l_{1}

The surface
S_{2A_{5}}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

The polystable degeneration of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}})
in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
is
(S_{2A_{5}},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
where
C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ}
is defined by the strict transform of the equations
(x_{2}^{2}-x_{0}^{3}x_{2}=0)
and
(x_{2}^{\prime 2}-x_{0}^{\prime 3}x_{2}^{\prime}=0)
on the irreducible components
S_{A_{5}}
and
S_{A_{5}}^{\prime}
, respectively. This is depicted in the lower right of Figure
6
.

Proof.

We construct an explicit degeneration of
(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},C_{2A_{5}})
to
(S_{2A_{5}},C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
by birationally modifying the constant family
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{C}):=(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},C_{2A_{5}})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. We perform several birational transformations of the family, modifying the central fiber to
(S_{2A_{5}},C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
. The central fibers of the transformations are depicted in Figure
6
. Let
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
denote the two
A_{5}
singularities in the central fiber
t=0
. Let
f_{1}
(resp.
f_{2}
) be the ruling of
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
through
p_{1}
(resp.
p_{2}
) contained in the curve
\mathcal{C}_{0}=C_{2A_{5}}
, and let
C
denote the remaining component. Suppose
p_{1}
(resp.
p_{2}
) is given by local coordinates
(y_{1},z_{1},t)
with local equation
y_{1}(y_{1}-z_{1}^{3})=0,t=0
(resp.
(y_{2},z_{2},t)
with equation
y_{2}(y_{2}-z_{2}^{3})=0,t=0
) and
f_{1}=(y_{1}=0)
(resp.
f_{2}=(y_{2}=0)
). Perform a
(3,1,1)
weighted blow-up of
\mathcal{S}
in the coordinates
(y_{i},z_{i},t)
of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, resulting in exceptional divisors
E_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
and
E_{2}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
. Denote this blow up by
\phi:\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\to\mathcal{S}
and denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
the strict transform of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
, so
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup E_{1}\cup E_{2}
. By direct computation, in a suitable choice of coordinates on
E_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)_{[x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}]}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cap E_{i}=(x_{1}=0)
and
\mathcal{C}|_{E_{i}}
has equation
x_{2}(x_{2}-x_{0}^{3})=0
.

The surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is the
(3,1)
weighted blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
in the two points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Let
\Delta_{i}=E_{i}|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}}
be the exceptional divisor over
p_{i}
. By construction,
(\Delta_{i})^{2}=-\frac{1}{3}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
has a
\frac{1}{3}(1,2)
singularity along
\Delta_{i}
. If
\tilde{f}_{i}
denotes the strict transform of
f_{i}
,
\tilde{f}_{i}
is contained in the smooth locus of
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
and by direct computation
(\phi|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}})^{*}f_{i}=\tilde{f}_{i}+3\Delta_{i}
, so
(\tilde{f}_{i})^{2}=-3
. Furthermore,
\phi^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{0})=\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}+E_{1}+E_{2}
, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cdot\tilde{f}_{i}=-1
. It follows that
\tilde{f}_{i}
is extremal in
\overline{NE}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
.

Let
F_{i}=f_{i}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\subset\mathcal{S}
and, as above, compute
\phi^{*}F_{i}=\tilde{F}_{i}+3\Delta_{i}
, where
\tilde{F}_{i}
is the strict transform of
F_{i}
. Then,
\tilde{F}_{i}\cdot\tilde{f}_{i}=-3
and
\phi|_{\tilde{F}_{i}}:\tilde{F}_{i}\to F_{i}
is the
(1,1)
-weighted blow up of
p_{i}\in F_{i}
, hence
(\tilde{f}_{i})^{2}=-1
where this intersection number is computed on the surface
\tilde{F}_{i}
. As
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}},\tilde{F}_{i})
is a plt pair and
(K_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}+\tilde{F}_{i})\cdot\tilde{f}_{i}=-1
,
\tilde{f}_{i}
is in fact a contractible extremal ray in
\overline{NE}(\widehat{\mathcal{S}})
and we may construct the flip of
\tilde{f}_{i}
for each
i
. Denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
the (relative) flip (over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
) of
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
. We construct the flip explicitly below.

By the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{f}_{i}/\tilde{F}_{i}}\to\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{f_{i}}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\to\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{F}_{i}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}|_{\tilde{f}_{i}}\to 0,

which by the computation above is

0\to\mathcal{O}(-1)\to\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{f_{i}}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\to\mathcal{O}(-3)\to 0,

we see that
\mathcal{N}_{\tilde{f_{i}}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-3)
(as
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}(-3),\mathcal{O}(-1))=0
).

In
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, blow up each curve
\tilde{f}_{i}
in a morphism
\phi_{1}:\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}
, which by the normal bundle computation yields exceptional divisors
\Sigma_{i,1}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
. By a similar computation, the negative section
\sigma_{i,1}
of
\Sigma_{i,1}
has normal bundle
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{i,1}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-2)
and by construction is not contained in the strict transform of
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
. Next, blow up each
\sigma_{i,1}
in a morphism
\phi_{2}:\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
which results in exceptional divisors
\Sigma_{i,2}\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
, with negative sections
\sigma_{i,2}
not contained in the strict transform of
\Sigma_{i,1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{i,2}/\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)\oplus\mathcal{O}(-1)
. Now, perform the Atiyah flop of each
\sigma_{i,2}
by blowing up each
\sigma_{i,2}
to create an exceptional divisor
\Sigma_{i,3}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and then contracting the opposite ruling. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}\dashrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{+}
be the Atiyah flop. The strict transform of
\Sigma_{i,2}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{+}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with normal bundle
\mathcal{O}(-2)
, so may be contracted to a
\frac{1}{2}(1,1,1)
singularity in a morphism
\phi_{2}^{+}:\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{+}\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
. Finally, the strict transform of
\Sigma_{i,1}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
with normal bundle
\mathcal{O}(-3)
(in the weighted coordinates on
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2)
) and hence may be contracted to a
\frac{1}{3}(1,1,2)
singularity in a morphism
\phi_{1}^{+}:\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
.

Denote by
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}
the image of
\Sigma_{i,3}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
. It is straightforward from the construction to see that the induced map
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\dashrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
is an isomorphism of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\setminus\{\tilde{f}_{1}\cup\tilde{f}_{2}\}
with
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}\setminus\{\tilde{f}_{1}^{+}\cup\tilde{f}_{2}^{+}\}
and that
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}
is contractible in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
. Furthermore, if
\tilde{F}_{i}^{+}
is the strict transform of
\tilde{F}_{i}
, by computation,
(K_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}}+\tilde{F}_{i}^{+})\cdot\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}=\frac{1}{3}
and hence by uniqueness of flips
[
KM98
, Corollary 6.4]
, the rational map
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\dashrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
is the flip of
\{\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\}

Now, observe that the rational map
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}\dashrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
restricted to
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
is the contraction of each
\tilde{f}_{i}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
, creating two
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
singularities on its strict transform
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
. Similarly, the restriction to
E_{i}
is the
(1,3)
weighted blow-up of
E_{i}
at the intersection point of
E_{i}
and
\tilde{f}_{i}
with exceptional divisor denoted
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}
, the flip of
\tilde{f}_{i}
. Denote by
E_{i}^{+}
the strict transform of
E_{i}
.

The surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
then has Picard rank 2 with two extremal rays,
[\Delta_{i}^{+}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cap E_{i}^{+}]
(both
i=1,2
are equivalent) and
[C^{+}]
, where
C^{+}
is the strict transform of
C
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
. Indeed, by computation,
(\Delta_{i}^{+})^{2}=0
and
(C^{+})^{2}=0
, yet
C^{+}\cdot\Delta_{i}^{+}=1
so both curves are extremal. Because
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cdot C^{+}=-2
,
[C^{+}]
is in fact extremal in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
, and as
(\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+},\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+})
is plt and
(K_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+})\cdot C^{+}=-2
,
[C^{+}]
can be contracted over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{+}
. Denote the image of this contraction by
\overline{S}
. This contraction glues the surfaces
E_{1}^{+}
and
E_{2}^{+}
together along
\Delta_{1}^{+}
and
\Delta_{2}^{+}
. In summary, the family
\overline{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
has generic fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and central fiber
E_{1}^{+}\cup E_{2}^{+}\cong S_{2A_{5}}
and the image of the family of curves
\mathcal{C}
has equation as claimed.

Finally, it is straightforward to see that
C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ}
has an
A_{5}
singularity in the smooth locus of each component of
S_{2A_{5}}
and
(S_{2A_{5}},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
is slc so indeed is a point of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{\mathrm{CY}}
. To verify the polystability, observe that
\Aut(S_{2A_{5}},C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
contains
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}
: on each component, we may consider the automorphism
[x_{0}:x_{1}:x_{2}]\mapsto[x_{0}:\lambda x_{1}:x_{2}]
of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,3)
. This fixes the curves
x_{2}(x_{2}-x_{0}^{3})=0
and
x_{1}=0
and thus extends to an automorphism of each component of
S_{2A_{5}}
fixing
C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ}
and the intersection of the two components. In particular, we have an independent
\mathbb{G}_{m}
action on each component of
S_{2A_{5}}
so
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}\subset\Aut(S_{2A_{5}},C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
. Therefore, by
[
ABB+23
, Proposition 8.11]
,
\Aut(S_{2A_{5}},C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{2}
and
(S_{2A_{5}},\frac{2}{3}C_{2A_{5}}^{\circ})
is polystable.
∎

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{blow up of }p_{1}\text{ and }p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{contraction of }\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}
